Fixed in milestone 50 (`93c437c`, `686baac`, `5d7ed13`, `8ac2b8d`, `c3bba17`, `0bbf4d7`, `65fdcab`): `tests/host/compat/devmodel.c` is the FAT host tests' device, with pending and durable writes: reads see every write, `block_flush` is the barrier that makes the pending writes durable or fails and keeps half of them, a stop refuses every write and barrier after it, and a snapshot at a stop is the medium with none, only the newest, or all of the writes pending since the driver's last barrier; the interruption matrix judges the three states at every stop of every sequence, walks each and mounts each read-only for the content oracle, then fails each barrier of a sequence in turn, and three durability rows require a file written and then fsynced, synced or unmounted to be on the medium with nothing pending kept. The model found the undo of a failed join in both drivers freeing the run while the file's tail still named it, a chain running into free clusters on the medium; the tail is ended again and flushed before the run is freed. The model also found FAT32 writing a directory's first cluster after the entry that published it, growing a directory before the run's zeros were on the medium, writing a rename's new name before the old one's slots were freed on the medium, freeing a removed directory's chain before its entry was gone, cutting a shrunk file's tail under a size that still covered it, and growing a file across a cluster with the join in the cache under a size that counted on it, and exFAT retiring a rename's old names after the new one was published, answering fsync from the cache, and with the same shrink and grow faults; each is a flush now, and `free_chain` in both drivers flushes before it frees a cluster. Codex's review of the model found the stop landing a write late, the checker's silence passing a state and exFAT's failure-plus-stop rows counting from the wrong write, each fixed; the model is atomic per request and settles a failed barrier's older half only, which the host Readme records as its limit. The drivers had twenty-eight barrier sites, not twenty-seven, and three failed no row when dropped one at a time: exFAT's closing sync in `free_chain`, which orders nothing and whose only effect is that the frees are on the medium when the call returns; FAT32's opening flush in `free_chain`, which every sequence reached either just after `fat_rmdir`'s own flush or with the unlinked file's size and first cluster still pending in the block the unlink rewrote; and `fat_rmdir`'s flush, which `free_chain` repeats with no write between the two. `notes.md` named two undo flushes that no longer existed, and the host Readme the two drivers' `free_chain` flushes, which is right for FAT32 and not for exFAT. The cache host test has the two interleavings on the fake device's callbacks: a block written, synced and dropped from the cache while a run read that covers it is inside the device, whose older staged copy the fill must not keep, and a `first` dirtied by another process while a sync's write of an `after` is inside the device, which the sync must write before its next `after`; the `write_gen` check dropped from the fill and `first_dirty` dropped from the sync each fail their row. The ext2 host test captures the write log's bytes, seeds every free block with stale bytes, replays the log on a snapshot of the disk and, at each write of a pointer block, requires every block it names to be allocated in the bitmap as it then stands and a named pointer block's contents as they then stand to be zeros or pointers to allocated blocks, under a cache of sixteen buffers and of sixty-four; it found a new pointer block's zeros marked `first` with the bitmaps, so that its pointers were `first` too and could be evicted before the bitmap that allocates the blocks they name, and the zeros are now written through and the pointers ordered after the bitmaps; the old marking and a plain write-back of the zeros each fail the row. `tests/run diskrate` reads `/proc/disk` before and after the exFAT and FAT32 rows and holds each row's disk commands and cache hits above zero and under bounds set from the measurement, the exFAT write under 9300 commands and 400000 hits, the FAT32 write under 14800 and 1300000, the small writes under 82000 and 120000 and 99600 and 350000, the exFAT files under 35500 and 65000; the hint's reversion in FAT32 and the batching's in exFAT each pass a bound by far. `tests/run queue` has the deadline's boundary on the lag device, whose period and periods `rach2.lagdeadline` and `rach2.lagperiods` now set, a lag of 75 ticks under a period of a hundred answered in time and one of 125 overdue once and taken; a request given up on at its fourth deadline, quarantined, its late completion freeing the slot, which the next request's wait shows with one slot in the pool; and a pool of two slots held by two throttled readers with a third reader that waits for a slot and is served; the release of a quarantined slot dropped and the pool's wait dropped each fail their row. `tests/run imagerace` builds a tree of its own, a program staged by `krffs/manifests/imagerace-tree.manifest` through a makefile in the Makefile's shape, changes the source, makes exactly the image and requires the new words and not the old, then makes it again and requires no rebuild. The rows for each site came on 2026-09-24 (milestone 06's close): exFAT's free-durability row requires a file of two clusters unlinked after its `fsync` to have both clusters free on the medium when the unlink returns, which is the closing sync's row; an unlink of a file its `fsync` put on the medium, in both matrices, is FAT32's opening flush's; and the crossing sequence is the row of the two barriers entry 173 added. `sh tests/host/fat_barriers_mutate.sh` drops each of the thirty sites in turn, fifteen in each driver, from a private copy and runs the driver's `--barriers` rows, and requires every site to fail a row but `fat_rmdir`'s flush, which no row can tell apart by construction and which the script names. The script did not complete at the close, since its run met entry 199; its first complete run, on the testing host on 2026-09-25, found entry 209, and with that entry's row it passed: every site but `fat_rmdir`'s flush fails a row when dropped. `fat32_hosttest.c` returned its failure count as its exit status; that is entry 174.

33. A USB stick takes one SCSI command per page (2026-09-11, milestone 50; the review's deferred driver item; severity: a slow result): the storage driver's request buffer is one page, `max_sectors` is left at zero and `MSC_MAX_SECTORS` is unused, so a 3.4 GiB save onto the sitting's stick is 900,000 commands; `rach2/sys/usb/xhci.c` describes a bulk transfer's data with one TRB carrying the buffer's address, so a 32 KiB buffer of heap pages that are not contiguous cannot be sent as it stands, though the request allocator already gives AHCI several heap pages. Reproduce with `tests/run diskrate`'s raw read of the stick and the commands per MiB in `/proc/disk`. It breaks nothing stated; the sitting's save and restore pay for it. Depends on nothing. Fixed, in phase 2h before the sitting, when a 32 KiB request goes to the stick as one command with page-aware TRBs, the ring's boundary, short and failed completions accounted, and the buffer's life held through a reset and a removal; when rows read and write patterned data through pages made non-contiguous on purpose, across ring and transfer boundaries, and pull the stick during the larger request; when a mutation that assumes contiguous pages fails them; and when commands per MiB and timed reads and writes of a stick are recorded, in QEMU and at the sitting on the machine. Required in phase 2h before the sitting. The session of 2026-09-11 deferred it on its own reading of the earlier wording, which is superseded; Codex's design review of that day is the requirement list phase 2h carries. Fixed in milestone 50 (`78f61743`, `f0569649`), phase 2h: the controller driver sends a request's pages as one transfer descriptor of chained Normal TRBs, one per page, with the TD size of xHCI 4.11.2.4, the first TRB published last behind barriers, one completion per descriptor, retirement only on a Success or Short event naming the last block, and records the stick driver holds (`xhci_td_hold`, `xhci_td_retired`, `xhci_td_release`); a failed block halts the endpoint or, for a block the controller could not run, puts it in the Error state; a halted endpoint is reset and moved on, a stopped or errored one moved on, through `usb_core_endpoint_quiesce`, which holds the command turn across Stop and Set TR Dequeue; Stopped events retire and deliver nothing; a control transfer completes at its status stage, whose failure overrides the data stage's answer; nothing is submitted on a halted or errored endpoint. The stick driver owns a request's pages from the data transfer's doorbell until the record is retired, stops the endpoint of an unretired transfer before completing the request and before the next command, stops bulk IN and bulk OUT before the class reset at a timeout, keeps an owned request at a pull until `msc_slot_closed`, which the core calls after Disable Slot's success and never on a refused or unanswered disable, reclaims the record when the disk layer and the controller have both let go, refuses the stick with the request kept on a stop, dequeue or clear refused or unanswered and hands it to the core, moves the control endpoint on after a stalled `Get Max LUN`, sends the device's `CLEAR_FEATURE(ENDPOINT_HALT)` after each controller-side clear, which no version of this driver had sent, retires and releases every command's record before its end is acted on, sets `max_sectors` to 64 and counts its data commands for `/proc/disk`. Proved by the rows of `xhci_hosttest`, `usb_hosttest` and `msc_hosttest`, each new check by a mutation, forty-eight in all, one not caught and recorded in `notes.md`; `tests/run usbdisk` writes and reads 4 MiB raw in 32 KiB requests as 128 commands each way, `tests/run usb` pulls a throttled stick under a 32 KiB read and reads the driver's lines, the request kept in its data stage and failed when the slot is given back, and `tests/run diskrate` times the stick's raw write and raw read of 4 MiB from the recovery kernel at exactly 128 commands each. Along the way: `xhci_command_issue` shifted the endpoint id out of the command TRB, so every endpoint command named endpoint zero, fixed. The commands per MiB and the timed reads and writes on the machine were taken at the sitting: the stick read raw at 33 MB/s in half-mebibyte commands, two a MiB, before entry 43's word copies and at 44 MB/s after them (`notes.md:115`, `:117`), the pull test read its first 64 MiB at 44 MB/s (`notes.md:119`), and after the second sitting's install it read raw at 43.3 MB/s and took a 64 MiB file at 21.7 MB/s (`notes.md:227`, `:238`).

34. Three smaller kernel costs, taken as measured experiments (2026-09-11, milestone 50; severity: a slow result): the pipe copies every byte four times through 512-byte stages with per-byte ring loops, and the installer hashes 1.7 GB through it at 9 MB/s where the same read without the pipe runs at 12, about 47 s of the sitting; `getrandom` of a large buffer loops in 256-byte pieces without giving way, since the timer's switch is taken at a call's exit and a call that does not return keeps the processor; the memory refusal runs after the bootstrap tables are placed, so a machine smaller than those tables resets before the named refusal. Fixed when the ring copies spans and the stages go only with the user-copy and wake contracts kept (partial failures, wrap, EOF, non-blocking, signals, the TinyCC-built kernel), the change kept only where a timed row shows the gain; when a bounded service point or a short return bounds one call, a heartbeat beside a large `getrandom` shows progress, and the bound removed fails the latency row; and when a bounded guard on the bootstrap allocation fits the allocator as it is, proved by a fixture that boots with too little usable memory and reports it, or the limit is written down as one. Fixed in milestone 50 for the second and the third cost (`93c73f73`): `getrandom` gives way every 4 KiB through `sched_service_point`, the switch the tick asked for taken between two pieces of the call; `keylatprobe random` puts the other tenant inside an eight-mebibyte `getrandom` made again as soon as it returns, and the latency row beside it requires the reader on the processor within two milliseconds of its wake, which the service point removed fails. The memory refusal was already the named one: the memory Readme records that `tests/run memory` boots the recovery pair at 8 MiB and requires the refusal, and the boot's own checks name the frame table's place and the managed minimum, so the limit is written down as one and nothing was added. The first cost, the pipe's copies, is required in phase 2h before the sitting, after entry 26's experiment, which Codex's review put before any judgement of the pipe's rate; the session of 2026-09-11 deferred both on its own, which is superseded. Fixed in milestone 50 (`bb31ddf2`), phase 2h item 3: the reader and the writer copy straight between the ring's spans and user memory, a user page at a time, a page that faults left in the ring with the bytes before it reported, the reader woken when the ring goes from empty to holding bytes and the writer once per read, the 512-byte stack buffers gone; proved by the ring's span rows in `pipe_ring_hosttest`, two rows more in `pipeprobe` (a read across an unmapped page, a write of three pages and a byte with the reader's acknowledgement before the close) and three mutations each failing its row; the pipe row's command on os-real fell from 7, 8 and 8 s to 6, 7 and 6, and the placement probe's far runs from 960 to 470 ms, so the change stays; the TinyCC-built kernel is `tests/run selfbuild` in item 4's sets. The latency row judges a count since entry 129: the reader beside the eight-mebibyte `getrandom` must be dispatched before 4096 more bytes of the call complete, and the microseconds are printed for the record. The memory limit is written down in `rach2/sys/mm/Readme.md`: a machine left with less than 1 MiB managed is refused by name, and `tests/run memory` boots the recovery pair at 8 MiB to that refusal.

35. A rename in KRFFS can be refused after it has removed its destination (2026-09-11, milestone 50; entry 15's remainder; severity: a wrong result, data lost): `krffs_rename_at` removes an existing destination before it changes the source's id, and the change is refused when the id would reach the medium in two units, the rule of entry 15, so a refused rename can leave neither name. Reproduce with a source whose id straddles a 4 KiB boundary and an existing destination, through `tests/run krffsmount`. It breaks the rename's contract that a refusal changes nothing. Depends on nothing. Fixed when a refusal leaves both names, proved first; then either a relocation that is safe across a cut or a written restriction of the layout, the old order the mutation, with the cuts of any relocation rows of their own. Fixed in milestone 50 (`629acc4d`): `krffs_rename_at` asks `krffs_write_is_whole` for the id it is about to write and for the parent, before it removes a destination of the same name, so a rename the rule refuses leaves both names and their bytes as they were; the host test places a file whose id field straddles a 4 KiB unit, gives it a destination with bytes, and requires the refusal, both names and the bytes, and with the check moved back after the removal the destination is gone.

36. The usb suite loses two rows under host load (2026-09-11, milestone 50; severity: a wrong result, no data lost): with ten spinning processes on the host, `tests/run usb` alone failed two rows and passed the rest, the monitor answering every `device_add`: in the boot with two hubs and fourteen devices behind them, the mouse at port 6.6 was never enumerated, the log ending at 5.8 with `usbprobe` exiting at 4.4 s and no line for 6.6 in between, so a hub's port change was lost or never read rather than read late; and the keystroke latency row saw `usb: latency: msi 0`, the key brought by the timer's pump before the controller's message arrived. Reproduce with `sh -c 'while :; do :; done' &` ten times and `tests/run usb`, once, 266 s. It breaks the enumeration's contract that every device present at boot is configured, and the latency row's that a message brings a keystroke. Depends on nothing. Fixed when the hub's port-change path is read under load and the lost change is named in a row that fails without the fix, and when the latency row either waits out the pump's turn or states that the pump may bring the key; run in phase 2g with entry 33's USB work. Fixed in milestone 50 (`4e0fc6a5`): the cause Codex's reading of the hub named and the slow-scan row confirmed: the hub's first scan told the core of each connected port as it read it, a device that settled while the scan went on had its reset asked for, `hub_port_reset_begin` refused a hub not yet running, and the core gave the port up; the scan now tells the core when it ends. `rach2.hubslow=MS` makes the scan wait between two ports, and `tests/run usb` boots the sixteen devices behind two hubs with 300 ms a port and requires every one configured, which the old order fails. The change bits are kept until their read or acknowledgement lands, and a short change report is read only as far as it came. The latency row takes the pump's key as well as the message's, judging the wake and the read for both and the message's stages for its own, since the pump may bring a key first and rows 9 to 11 prove the messages. A race older than this change stays: a port scanned as disconnected that connects during the scan has its connection change acknowledged with the scan's bits and its status not read again; it is not the loaded host's case and was left with a note. Disposed of in phase 6: the race is real, and the lines that decide it are `hub.c`'s scan branch, which records the change word it read, and `clear_next`, which sends the acknowledgement a tick or more later, when the hub clears the latch as it then stands; a device that lands in between has its connection change cleared unread, and the core's pass over hub ports (`core.c:1589`) reads the driver's recorded status, sees disconnected and skips the port for good. The driver now reads a port's status once more after acknowledging its change bits, the Readme says so, and a row in `tests/host/hub_hosttest.c` puts a device on a port the instant after its status is read; the row fails when the re-read is not asked for.

37. The mount suite's dirty boot lost to the load on its deadline (2026-09-10, milestone 50, phase 2f's set; severity: a wrong result, no data lost): in the pool of twenty the row's machine printed `LAGPROBE OK` at 65 s of its own clock and was stopped by the harness's 180 s deadline, the emulator under twenty machines running at about a third of its rate alone; alone the row takes a minute. The row was first read as the probe leaving too little dirty, which was wrong: KRFFS syncs a file's data as it is written, what the reclaim writes back is the volume's metadata, and the row's pattern accepts one frame written back, which is its contract. Reproduce with `tests/run mount` in a full set at twenty. It breaks nothing of the kernel's. Depends on entry 2. Fixed when the set runs at a width under which the row meets its deadline, which phase 2g's set at twelve measures, or the row's deadline is set from the guest's own clock. Fixed in milestone 50 (`0aee59ef`): the row's deadline is 400 s, set from the emulator's rate in a pool, after the set at twelve lost it as the set at twenty had; mount passed alone in 293 s after.

38. A kernel record can begin inside a program's console write (2026-09-10, milestone 50, phase 2f's set; severity: a wrong result, no data lost): `klog` ends a line a program left open before it writes a record, so a record raised by an interrupt during a write of `AHCIPROBE OK` split the token into `AHC`, the record and `IPROBE OK`, and the harness never saw it; alone the probe's three lines are out before the lag device's duplicate completion two ticks later, in the pool of twenty they were not. Reproduce with `tests/run queue` in a full set. It breaks the harness's contract that a token a program prints in one write is one line of the log. Depends on nothing. Fixed when a record raised while a console write is in progress waits in `klog`'s queue, as one raised inside another record's delivery does, and is written when the write ends, the write being each chunk of at most 256 bytes that `sys_write` hands the tty, so a token printed within one chunk is one line of the log and a record waits for at most that chunk; the klog host test raises a record between two bytes of a bracketed write and requires the bytes together and the record after them, and the bracket removed fails that row. Fixed in milestone 50 (`2f8cddf5`): `console_write_begin` and `console_write_end` bracket the console tty's write of each chunk, a record raised inside waits in the queue, and the klog host test's row fails with the bracket dropped; the queue suite passed in the sets at twenty and at twelve after.

39. `mshowfat` aborts at random on the build host (2026-09-11, milestone 50; severity: a run lost at its preparation): mtools 4.0.49 on macOS 26.6 aborted in `mshowfat` on roughly one call in three, and on one run in six of the disk builder's five calls, with nothing on stderr and a corrupted path in what it did print (`::/boot_/kernel`), so `prepare-all` failed before any suite ran; the same binary had run some fifty calls without a fault earlier that day. Reproduce with `mshowfat -i p1.img ::/boot/fallback` in a loop of twenty on a boot partition the builder made. It breaks the rule that a set starts when the tree builds. Depends on nothing. Fixed in milestone 50 (`4df6bcc6`): `tools/image/fatchains.c`, a reader of a FAT16 or FAT32 image's directory and chain, prints the records the builder's chain check parses, built by the builder beside `mkmbr`; `mshowfat` is no longer required by the registry or reported by `doctor.sh`.

40. The block cache could take the whole kernel heap on a machine with memory (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: the recovery system wedged, no data lost): `bcache_limit` was half of `mem_cache_target`, an eighth of the machine's frames, and each buffer is a page of the kernel heap, whose size is `KVA_HEAP_SIZE`, 256 MiB, whatever the memory; on the Lenovo's 2.7 GB the limit was 339 MiB, so the sitting's save, 1852 ranges written into the stick's exFAT volume, filled the heap with dirty buffers during the first range, and every fork after it failed for want of a 16 KiB stack, so every range's `dd` reported failure and the recovery system could start no process, not even `reboot`; the disk was read and not written. Reproduce in QEMU with `-m 3072`: the recovery system writing 512 MiB into an exFAT file logs `kheap: 1 pages: no room or no frame` without end and does not return. It breaks the recovery system's promise to run on the machine it repairs, and every 512 MiB machine hides it, since there the limit is 64 MiB. Depends on nothing. Fixed the same day (`bcache.c`): the limit is also capped at half the heap's pages, 32768 buffers; with it the same 3 GB machine writes two 512 MiB files with the cache at 128 MiB, the heap at 33909 pages and fork working after each; `tests/run diskrate` passes with its counts unchanged. The unfixed kernel was the mutation, measured above. The reviewers read it at the milestone's close, as the plan says of a fix the machine produced. The cap has a row since 2026-09-24 (milestone 06's close): the last row of `tests/host/bcache_cost_hosttest.c` stands for a machine of 3 GiB, `host_cache_target` 196608 frames in `tests/host/compat/pressure_host.c`, writes 33024 blocks, 256 past half the heap's pages, and requires the cache to stop at 32768 buffers and write 256 back; `bcache_mutate.sh`'s `cap` fault takes the cap out of `bcache_limit` and the row fails.

41. The machine's network ran on the timer tick (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: a slow result, 400 KB/s where the link is a gigabit): `rach2/sys/network/driver/rtl8168.c` is polled, its interrupt mask stays zero and `netdev_poll` runs its receive routine from the timer tick, so an acknowledgement reaches TCP only on the next 10 ms boundary; the send and receive rings of a connection are one page (`TCP_RING_SIZE`, `TCP_WINDOW_SIZE` 4096), so at most 4 KiB is in flight per boundary, 409.6 KB/s out, and inward the Mac's segments are acknowledged 9 ms after they arrive with a 4 KiB window. Measured on the Lenovo: `dd if=/dev/zero bs=1M | nc` to the Mac 400 KB/s, the same over SSH and the same figure milestone 43 recorded and worked around with the stick; from the Mac 95 KB/s by `nc` timing and 292 KB/s by the capture's own clock, a difference the record keeps; the captures on the Mac (`tcpdump -tt`) show the acknowledgement of a whole 4 KiB burst 1.3 ms after it and the next burst 10.0 ms after the first, burst after burst. Reproduce on the machine with the commands above; QEMU cannot, since its rtl8139 and e1000 drivers take interrupts and it has no RTL8168. It breaks nothing stated and makes the sitting's second copy over the wire two and a half hours for 3.44 GiB and the acceptance's pull test read at that rate. Depends on nothing. Fixed when the driver takes the card's messages through `pci_msi_enable` on a vector of its own with a handler registered before the enable, the sources unmasked only then, the tick kept as the fallback pump, a storm bounded and counted, the counters on the diagnostic line; when the rings are 64 KiB with the advertised window clamped to 65535 everywhere it is computed, so that an empty ring never advertises zero; when a transmit the driver refuses is kept in the ring and sent at the next pump rather than lost to a retransmission; when the host models prove each of these and the machine measures both directions with useful traffic between tick boundaries. Fixed the same night (`3f525313`): the driver takes the card's messages on a vector of its own, the handler registered before `pci_msi_enable` and the sources unmasked after it, the status acknowledged before the rings are drained and the handler bounded at two rounds, a bound reached with work still announced acknowledged once more and counted as deferred, a source the acknowledgement leaves set with nothing in the rings for sixteen entries in a row taken for a storm and masked, the tick's poll kept as the fallback with its work counted; the rings are 64 KiB with the window clamped through `tcp_rcv_window`; a transmit the driver refuses with -ENOBUFS stays in the ring for the next acknowledgement or the tick, which pumps four connections a tick in rotation and gives up a connection refused for thirty seconds, while any other error counts the segment as sent for the retransmission path; `ethernet_tx` returns the driver's answer and `ip_tx` -ENOBUFS. Codex read the change twice and eleven faults of the two readings are in the rows: the host models prove the enable order by the log's index, an arrival during the drain, the deferred case past both rounds' budget, the storm after sixteen stuck entries and not after an entry that did work, the tick's counted work, the refused card, the clamp, an 80000-byte stream across the ring's end compared byte for byte, a refusal with a segment outstanding, a permanent error, the give-up, a zero window over a refused segment kept, and the rotation reaching the last of six; fifteen mutations fail them. On the Lenovo with the stick rewritten: vector 51 for the adapter in /proc/interrupts, 64 MiB to the Mac in 5.8 s (11.6 MB/s) by `nc` and 6.0 s over SSH, 8 MiB from the Mac in 0.72 s (11.7 MB/s) by the capture's clock and 64 MiB over SSH in 6.4 s, no retransmission and no acknowledgement gap over 1 ms in the capture, which is the wire: the Mac's own port negotiated 100 Mbit full duplex. The `nc` inbound figures of 95 KB/s and 0.5 MB/s were the Mac's `nc -w 120` waiting for the far end to close, as the capture showed, and the old inbound rate by the capture's clock was 292 KB/s.

42. Every disk path moves one 32 KiB command at a time (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: a slow result): `BLOCK_REQ_MAX_SECTORS` is 64, the AHCI, ATA and stick drivers each declare it, the cache fills runs of eight blocks and the raw device path batches 64 sectors (`DEVFS_RAW_BATCH_SECTORS`), so a read is one 32 KiB command in flight and the next after its completion. Measured on the Lenovo: the Samsung 870 EVO reads 84 MB/s raw at `bs=1M` and 82 at `bs=32k`, 2600 commands a second where the drive reads 500 MB/s; the SanDisk stick reads 24 MB/s raw and takes a file write at 8.5 MB/s; the save of 3.44 GiB took 527 s. Reproduce with `dd` on the machine; in QEMU the same paths measure 16 to 78 MB/s bounded by the emulator, so no test has seen the per-command cost. It breaks nothing stated; the sitting's install and grow are budgeted at ten minutes and the milestone's load rows pay for it. Depends on entry 41 for its measurements over the wire and on nothing else. Fixed when a request may be up to 2048 sectors on AHCI, whose command table then holds a PRDT entry per page, and up to 1024 on the stick, whose transfer records and ring are proved to hold the TRBs, with the cache's runs and the raw batch sized to the device's limit within a memory budget, every host model walking the scattered pages with its own pattern, the QEMU rows issuing calls of the new sizes and counting commands, the sizes swept on the machine (256, 1024 and 2048 on the SSD; 256 and 1024 on the stick) and the smallest size at the plateau chosen; more than one command in flight is a change of its own and not this entry's. Fixed the same night (`3fee983e`): the core's limit is 2048 sectors and `block_max_sectors` holds every driver to it, the AHCI disk declaring 2048 with a command table of 264 PRDT entries, the stick 1024 with transfer records of 130 blocks allocated per endpoint as it opens and a transfer that would wrap the ring refused before publication, the ATA disk 64 of its own; a request the heap has no buffer for is asked for again at half the size down to a page's, a read's bytes are copied out with interrupts on, the cache's runs are the device's limit within half the cache with the caller's block kept from the victim's choice, the raw batch is the least of the call, the device and 1 MiB with eight heap batches out at once, the fault-safe path is called in the device's pieces, and `sectorcopy` gathers a mebibyte before each write. Codex read the change twice and thirteen findings of the two readings are in the code and the rows: the AHCI model describes a mid-page mebibyte as 257 entries and refuses 266 with no command issued, the xHCI model moves a 512 KiB transfer of 129 blocks page by page and refuses 131 pieces with the ring untouched, the stick model takes a 1024-sector request as one command with one 512 KiB transfer and the medium's bytes, the cache fills a run of 256 on a device of 2048 and half a small cache with the caller's block kept, `diskrate` counts the stick's 8 commands for 4 MiB at 512 KiB a request and the AHCI disk's 32 for 32 MiB at 1 MiB and 1024 at 32 KiB with the bytes compared, and the authority probe's raw checks are one call and one flush. On the Lenovo: the SSD reads 512 MiB at 93.8 MB/s in 512 commands of 1 MiB, 92.8 at 512 KiB, 89.5 at 128 KiB and 78.5 at 32 KiB in 8192 commands, so the command count no longer bounds it and the kernel's byte-wise copies do, entry 43; the stick reads 32.8 MB/s in 512 KiB commands against 23.7 in 32 KiB ones, and a file write onto its exFAT volume runs at 12.5 MB/s against 8.5. The AHCI handler's synchronization of a mebibyte's cache lines was left in the handler and not measured apart. The queue suite, not run after this change, failed when it next ran on 2026-09-12: its late-answers row throttled a disk to 16 KiB a second against 32 KiB requests, and the cache's run of 1 MiB outlasted the four periods and was declared stalled; the row is set against the 1 MiB request at 512 KiB a second (`a3aff958`).

43. The kernel's copies bound the disk at 90 MB/s (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: a slow result): with a mebibyte per command the SSD reads at 93.8 MB/s whatever the command size, 512 KiB or 128 KiB the same within five percent, where the drive reads at 500 MB/s and the stick at 32.8, so the bound is no longer the command count but the copies: `k_memcpy` and `k_memset` in `rach2/sys/lib/kstring.c` are byte loops, the kernel is built without optimisation, and a raw read passes each byte through two of them, the request's page to the batch and the batch to the user, with `copy_to_user`'s own loop. Reproduce on the machine with `dd if=/dev/disk0 bs=1M` against `bs=32k` from /proc/disk's counts, 94 against 78 MB/s; in QEMU the emulator's rate hides it. It breaks nothing stated; it costs the sitting's install of 1655 MiB twenty seconds and every copy of the machine's the same tax, and it is the mechanism entry 26's slow store path was amplified by. Depends on nothing. Fixed when the copies move a word at a time through `rep movsl` and `rep stosl` with the tails in bytes, in both compilers' dialect since the machine builds the kernel with TinyCC, proved on the host tests of the string functions with lengths and alignments around the word, and the SSD's raw read measured again with the commands counted. Fixed the same day (`6c41e9fb`): on the kernel's own target `k_memcpy` and `k_memset` move the words through the string instructions after clearing the direction flag, which a user program may leave set at a system call, and the byte loop takes the tail of up to three; `k_memmove`'s forward case is the copy; on any other target the byte loop is the whole function. The string host test sweeps every length to 67 at every alignment of both buffers for the copy, the fill and the move with a sentinel on each side, and two mutations fail it; the pipe probe sweeps the same lengths and alignments through the kernel, which the default suite runs, and a mutation that drops the tail for lengths of 60 to 67 alone fails it there; the style, host, queue, default and self-build suites pass, the last building the kernel with TinyCC. Measured on the Lenovo with this kernel on 2026-09-12: the SSD reads 328 MB/s raw, 512 commands for 512 MiB, where it read 94 MB/s before, and the stick 44 MB/s where it read 33 (`notes.md:115`, `:117`).

44. A lost burst came back one segment per timeout (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: a stalled transfer, no data lost): `rach2/sys/network/tcp/tcp.c` sent the oldest unacknowledged segment again once per expiry of the retransmission timer and nothing else, ignored duplicate acknowledgements, doubled the timeout at each expiry and, taking a round-trip sample only when the timer was off, never shortened it in a stream, so a run of n segments lost together came back at one segment per timeout with the timeout at its 60 s ceiling from the seventh, 16 segments in half an hour, each acknowledged by the Mac within a millisecond. Measured on the Lenovo with entry 41's kernel: the capture on the Mac of the session on port 57352 shows the notebook's segments 1467826038 to 1467849599 never arriving, the Mac's duplicate acknowledgements of 1467826038 answered with nothing, and the resends at 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 60 and 60 s; the listing on the notebook read `cwnd=2920 flight=48015`. Six such moments in fifteen minutes of copying, entry 45. Reproduce in the host test with a burst the peer never acknowledges. It broke the sitting's second copy: ten of 1852 ranges cut short, the copy of 3.44 GiB running for hours. Depends on nothing. Fixed the same day (`e5807121`): the third duplicate acknowledgement in a row sends the segment the peer lacks again at once with the congestion window halved plus the three segments the duplicates say have left, each further duplicate adds a segment, and each partial acknowledgement sends the next hole (RFC 5681, RFC 6582); a timeout begins a recovery that sends the unacknowledged data again from the oldest byte, a segment more per acknowledgement from a window of one, in every state, with the tick pumping a resend the driver refused; a round-trip sample runs one at a time on data not sent again and ends at the acknowledgement that covers it, so the timeout a loss doubled is measured again from the first new data acknowledged; `/proc/net/tcp`'s `retrnsmt` column counts the segments sent again. The rows: seventeen segments lost whole come back in six acknowledgements with no tick after the first timeout, in order, each once, and the doubled timeout returns to its minimum; one hole is sent again at the third duplicate and not at the second, with the window at the threshold plus three and a duplicate of the ending acknowledgement beginning nothing; two holes, the second at the partial acknowledgement; a close over a lost burst recovered through `FIN_WAIT_1` with a refused resend sent by the tick; a sample that the acknowledgement of an earlier segment does not end. Six mutations fail them; the style, host, socket, ssh and procfiles suites pass.

45. Runs of the notebook's frames are lost between its card and the Mac (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting; severity: a slow result with entry 44 fixed, a stalled one before): in fifteen minutes of copying at 11 MB/s the capture on the Mac shows six moments at which a run of consecutive segments the notebook sent never arrived, 16, 6, 8, 4, 6 and 1 segments, twice with a second hole a few segments later, the segments after each hole delivered. The notebook counted no refused transmit, no transmit drop, no receive error and no drop; the Mac's interface counted no input error; the Mac's port negotiated 100 Mbit full duplex, and `rtl8168.c` advertises 1000 full duplex with 10 and 100 and no pause (`R8168_ADVERTISE_VALUE 0x01e1`, `R8168_CTRL1000_VALUE 0x0200`), so the notebook's port links at 1000 Mbit to the router's switch, whose port to the Mac is ten times slower and which, with no pause advertised, drops what its buffer cannot hold. The driver read none of the card's own tallies and did not log link changes, so the kernel could not say whether the card, the wire or the switch lost the frames. Reproduce: a copy of the save's ranges over SSH with `tcpdump` on the Mac; QEMU cannot, having no RTL8168 and a link that loses nothing. It breaks nothing stated; with entry 44 a loss costs a round trip at the third duplicate or a second at a timeout. Depends on entry 44 for the cost of a loss. Fixed when the port advertises pause in both directions as the reference does, so the switch holds the port instead of dropping, and each link change the card reports is logged with the speed, duplex and pause resolved; when the driver reads the chip's tally block on demand and reports it through a `counters` operation into `/proc/net/dev`'s columns as Linux fills them, transmit errors, underruns, collisions and the frames the card gave up on, missed frames, alignment errors and multicasts, proved on the host model with a chip that answers, one that never does and a stopped card; and when the copy is run again on the notebook with the new kernel, the link's line read from the log, and the file's columns read at each retransmission the listing's `retrnsmt` column reports, which places any loss that remains in the card or beyond it. Fixed in the kernel the same day (`f570e6bb`, `2832695a`): the counters, the advertisement written before negotiation restarts, the link log, and the handler counting the messages that carry the transmit and receive error sources before the acknowledgement loses them, with rows and mutations for each; Codex read the findings at the owner's word and asked for the error sources counted and the advertisement's order proved. On the Lenovo with that kernel: the link line reads `link 1000 Mb/s, full duplex, pause both ways`, and the Mac's port, which had been at 100 Mbit, negotiates `1000baseT` once its cable is replaced; the owner found that cable links a second machine at 100 Mbit too, so the mismatch was the cable's, and the loss is placed: the router's port toward the Mac, ten times slower than the notebook's, dropped what its buffer would not hold, with no pause to ask for. The copy of the 1852 ranges then ran to its end in 498 s, 14 MB/s in the long ranges, no retransmission in the listing, no drop and no error in the card's columns beyond one transmit error from before the link came up, and every range hashes as the disk does; the rate is the kernel's byte loops between the disk and the card, entry 43. Then the owner put the slow cable back into the Mac: the Mac's port at 100 Mbit, the notebook's at 1000 as before, and range-0, 1.74 GB, copied in 163 s at the slow wire's rate with no segment sent again, no drop and no error in the card's columns, the file hashing as the disk does; the same range under the same mismatch lost a burst every few minutes on the kernel before, so the pause the port now advertises is what holds the router. Closed on the machine.

46. A megabyte's heap allocation fails beside a full cache (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting's install; severity: a slow result and a flooded console, no data lost): since entry 42 the raw device path takes its batches from the heap a megabyte at a time (`raw_batch_take`, `DEVFS_RAW_BATCH_SECTORS` 2048), and the cache's run stage the same, while the cache itself holds up to half the heap's pages one page at a time and returns them in no order; once the installer has hashed the 1.7 GB image the heap holds 32768 scattered cache pages and no 256 contiguous free ones, so every batch's allocation fails, `kheap` logs `256 pages: no room or no frame` at error level for each try, and the request goes through at half the size, halved again as needed. Measured on the Lenovo: the installer's write of 1735393280 bytes took two minutes instead of the SSD's twelve seconds, 167880 disk commands for the first attempt's 1.7 GB, 10 KiB a command, and the console showed the line thirty times a second for the whole write. Reproduce in QEMU with a 3 GB machine: hash a file larger than the cache's limit, then `reflash` a raw device with megabyte writes. It breaks nothing stated; the halving is what keeps it a slow result rather than a failure. Depends on entry 42. Fixed when the raw batches and the run stage are taken from a pool made before the cache can fragment the heap, at boot or at the path's first use, and kept, so that no request-time allocation competes with the cache's pages, and when an allocation a caller can halve is asked for quietly, the log line reserved for one nothing can satisfy; proved by a host row that fills a cache with single pages and asks for a megabyte, and by the installer suite's rate with the cache full. Fixed the same day, in the heap rather than in each caller, since the request layer allocates its own pages as the raw path does: the top 16 MiB of the heap's region is a reserve of sixteen runs of 256 pages that allocations under 64 pages never enter, and a run of 64 pages or more that the rest of the region cannot give comes from there and goes back when freed; the reserve costs no frame until a run is taken (`kheap.c`). The new host test of the heap drives the page map with the frames replaced by counters: single pages fill the region up to the reserve and no further; with every second page freed, so that no run of two remains, sixteen runs of 256 pages come from the reserve, the seventeenth is refused, one given back is given again, and single pages still do not enter it; a run with room below the reserve is taken below it; a shortage of frames inside a run gives the run back. Three mutations fail these rows; the style, host and memory suites pass. On the Lenovo after the load row, the heap filled and scattered by three minutes of builds in four copies: the kernel before the reserve read 256 MiB of the root's partition raw in 8192 commands of 32 KiB at 47 MB/s and added 133 failure lines to its log; the kernel with it, put in the candidate slot through `bootctl` for one trial and then committed, read the same in 256 commands of 1 MiB at 174 MB/s and added none. Closed on the machine.

47. The disk's longest delay reads as a wrapped negative (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the sitting's load; severity: a wrong number in the record, nothing else): `/proc/stat` printed `latency_disk_us 4294963059` on the installed Lenovo, 4237 microseconds below zero, and the figure changed under load to other values just below zero, so it is not one bad reading but a recurring one: `request.c` and `ahci.c` note a request's delay as `pit_now_us() - request->taken_us` with no guard, and the microsecond clock is read later than it was at the taking and still answers less, a backwards step within one tick, which the unsigned subtraction turns into the largest delay ever seen and `latency_note` keeps as the maximum. Reproduce on the machine by reading `/proc/stat` after any disk activity; QEMU's clock may not step the same way. It breaks the load row's record, whose disk figure this is. Depends on nothing. Fixed when a delay whose end reads before its start is taken as zero, or the clock is made monotonic across a tick's boundary, proved by a host row on the latency record with a backwards clock and by the load row's disk figure on the machine reading as a delay. Fixed at the milestone's close: `latency_span` takes the two readings and drops a pair whose later reading is the smaller, and the request's completion, the AHCI completion, the console's line and the timer's tick all go through it; `tests/host/latency_hosttest.c` drives the record with a clock that steps back, the AHCI host test checks that a completion notes its delay from the request's taking, and three mutations fail these rows. Read on the Lenovo after the second sitting's install: `latency_disk_us` read 33580, a delay and not a wrapped negative, and five sessions building for ten minutes left a longest disk delay of 14.3 ms, against 4.2 ms on a fresh boot (`notes.md:230`, `:240`). Read again on 2026-09-24 after that day's kernel install: `latency_disk_us 4413`.

48. `chown` takes a directory's set-id bits everywhere but the boot root (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the second sitting, corrected after review; severity: a wrong result, no escalation): the tree's own rule is `cred_kill_setid` at `syscall.c:1236`, which leaves a directory's bits alone and clears setgid only when group-execute is set, and `syscall.c:1258` applies it on a write and a truncate. The path chown and the descriptor chown cleared both bits on any owner change with neither test, at `01d02656`'s `syscall.c:4700` and `:4905`; both now call `vfs_chown_plan`, which is at `syscall.c:4652` with its callers at `:4734` and `:4922`. The narrow path is the boot root's alone: `syscall.c:1330` tests both root identity and file system type, so a mounted KRFFS volume takes the wide path too. On the installed machine, whose root is ext2, `mkdir d; chmod 4755 d; chown 1000:1000 d` leaves `0755` and `chmod 2700 f; chown 1000:0 f` leaves `0700`. This is the tree contradicting itself, not POSIX: POSIX requires the bits cleared on a regular file for an unprivileged caller and leaves the rest implementation-defined, so either rule is allowed, but one kernel may not hold both. `tests/modeprobe.c:536` states the intended rule and fails on an ext2 root. Reproduce with those two shell lines on any mounted volume. Depends on nothing. Fixed when both chowns apply the same two tests the boot root's path applies, proved by a host row over the vfs path and by `modeprobe` on an ext2 root; that probe also needs `syscall.c:4685`, which refuses a chown that changes nothing before it asks whether anything changed, which is a second mismatch of the same kind. Not claimed: that a setgid directory passes its group on here. `ext2.c:2034` gives a new inode the caller's effective group, not the parent's. Closed in phase 6 (`db580d84`, `40b6f436`) by `modeprobe` over both dispatch paths on an ext2 root, and since 2026-09-24 also by the host row the entry asked for: `sh tests/host/chown_mutate.sh` extracts `vfs_chown_plan` and the credential helpers it calls from a private copy of `syscall_fs.c` into `tests/host/fixtures/chown_plan.c`, and requires that a directory keeps its set-id bits, setuid goes with any change of owner or group, setgid only when group-execute is set beside it, root gives a file to anyone, an owner changes only the group and only to one of its own, a caller that does not own the file changes nothing, and a request that changes nothing needs no right; a directory losing its bits, setgid cleared whatever group-execute says, an owner allowed to give a file away and a group change judged by the file's own group each fail their row. The line numbers above are `01d02656`'s; the plan is now `vfs_chown_plan` at `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c:4266`, called at `:4356` and `:4558`.
49. A small write to a file costs six disk commands and an append twelve (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the second sitting, corrected after review; severity: a slow result): measured on the installed machine, 200 MiB written in 1 MiB calls runs at 66.7 MB/s and 78 MiB in 4 KiB calls at 494 KB/s, 8.1 ms a call; 2000 such calls are 23996 disk commands of which 11998 are cache flushes. Broken down: a 4 KiB rewrite in place is six commands and three flushes, an append or a write that grows the file twelve and six. Where they come from, as far as the code shows: one write call is one ext2 batch (`ext2.c:2227` to `:2255`), and after the batch the inode is stored twice, at `:2260` and again at `:2268` once the size and times are updated, each store reaching `bcache_write` at `:973` which writes the buffer there and then; allocation adds the bitmap and descriptor at `:1281` and `:1286` and an indirect mapping a pointer block at `:1646`. Each write command is followed by a cache flush in the driver, `ahci.c:1136` and `ata.c:289`, which is the write-through contract `devfs.c:182` states for the raw window. Not claimed: that `bcache_sync` flushes the drive. It does not; `bcache.c:895` writes dirty buffers and issues no flush of its own. Withdrawn: a figure of 0.53 ms for a bare flush, since `ext2.c:2164` makes `fsync` a `bcache_sync` and with nothing dirty it issues no command at all, so what that measurement timed is unknown. Also unproved: that the ten minutes `rach2-install`'s grow takes are this same cost. `resize2fs` writes through the raw window, where a 4 KiB call is already one command, so batching in ext2 cannot touch it and the grow needs its own per-phase measurement. It breaks no stated contract. Depends on nothing. Fixed when a small write costs what the medium costs, which needs first a trace of each command's role, size and latency for the four cases above, and then a design that either lets a batch span calls or stops storing the inode twice. A batch that spans calls must prove, before it is taken: that data and zeroing are durable before a size exposes them; that `setattr`, rename and truncate cannot publish pending state; that a freed and reused block leaves no older write aimed at its new owner; that dirty inode state survives close; and that eviction, sync, unmount and device loss keep the dependencies, with delayed errors still observable. The cache's `first` and `after` marks order a bitmap before a pointer but do not order two `after` buffers and do not bind a direct `bcache_write`, so they are not enough on their own. Proved by the host rows that replay pointer publication and interruption, `ext2_hosttest.c:2032`, `:2072` and `:2922`, and `bcache_hosttest.c:464` and `:893`, with cross-call cases added, and by `tests/run ext2` and `tests/run queue` unchanged. Root cause and fix, 2026-09-13: the second store is `ext2.c:2268`, which rewrites the inode after the size and times are set, where `:2260` has already written it. Removing the first takes a rewrite from six commands to four and an append from twelve to ten. Taken, with the ext2 contract rewritten to match. Measured on the machine: 2000 writes of 4 KiB cost 19996 commands and 9998 flushes against 23996 and 11998, and the rate of 4 KiB writes rose from 494 to 911 KB/s. The grow's ten minutes are a second and separate defect, which is entry 53. The cross-call batch is taken in `aecb6f1b`. [ext2-write-batch-result.md](ext2-write-batch-result.md) records its ownership, ordered publication, delayed errors, native mutation proofs and command-count proof: sixteen rewrites cost four commands and two appends cost six, against 64 and twelve with the per-call drain restored. The runs were made here on the testing host: `tests/run ext2 queue diskrate default selfbuild-ext2 host` passes, the last of them on the kernel the machine built. The Lenovo has run the batch since the kernel of `b984b33c` was installed on 2026-09-21: `e2fsck -f -n` found that ext2 root consistent on 2026-09-21, and entry 161 is the fault the batch met there on 2026-09-24 (`dev-log/06-review/results-2026-09-20.md`). Entry 157 is the regression the batch brought, and its fix. This entry is closed. The machine's counts, taken on the Lenovo on 2026-09-25 by `manual_probes.sh`'s `writes` mode on the final kernel, `4ae77083`, before the root was replaced for entry 223: 2000 appended calls of 4 KiB through one open file cost 124, 120 and 120 disk commands and 62, 60 and 60 flushes in three runs, and 2000 rewrites in place 54 commands and 27 flushes in each.
50. The loopback ring drops under any load and logs a line per drop (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the second sitting; severity: a wrong result and a record destroyed): The loopback ring had eight slots and logged a `KLOG_WARN` for each datagram refused when it was full, at `01d02656`'s `ip.c:174` and `:250`, draining at most 32 a tick at `:318`; it held 48 at the close of phase 6 and holds 64 now, `LOOP_RING_SLOTS` at `ip.c:186`, refusing at `:305` and draining at most `LOOP_RING_SLOTS * 4` an invocation. A local TCP connection to the machine's own address fills the ring at once, so a bulk transfer stalls and every refusal writes a line; the kernel log wrapped within seconds, taking the boot log with it (`klog.c:185` evicts the oldest), and `metal_accept.sh:269` and `:317` read that boot log for the USB inventory and the local APIC, so the acceptance failed two rows about the boot. After a reboot it passes 71 rows. A small exchange works. Refusal is not uniform: `ip.c:249` returns `ENOBUFS` and `tcp.c:290` keeps the refused payload without advancing, but the control path at `tcp.c:191` drops the answer on the floor, so an acknowledgement built while the ring is full is simply lost, which is the first thing to measure. Reproduce on the machine with `nc -l -p 5561 > /dev/null &` and `dd if=/dev/zero bs=1M count=100 | nc 127.0.0.1 5561`. It breaks the record `/dev/bootlog` is for. Depends on nothing. Fixed when a local transfer completes and the boot log survives it, counting refusals instead of logging each one. Back pressure cannot be a wait inside `ip_tx`, whose callers may be interrupt handlers with interrupts off (`net.h:20`); it has to be outside that path. Proved by a loopback transfer of 100 MiB that completes, by a boot log that still holds its first line after it, and by the refusal counter rising where the log used to. Measured on the Lenovo, as the owner reported after installing `e1eec90c`; the logs do not record which kernel ran: eight 256 MiB transfers through the loopback each delivered 268435456 bytes with `loopback_dropped` at zero and `loopback_peak` at 47, and the retained log began with its first record, `boot: Rach2 Booting`, and held no loopback line (`notes.md:591-604`, `:636`). This entry is closed. The refusal has its row since 2026-09-24 (milestone 06's close): `test_loop_full` in `tests/host/packet_hosttest.c` fills the 64 slots, sends 192 more datagrams and requires `-ENOBUFS` for each, `ip_loop_dropped()` at 192, the ring at 64 with its peak at 64, exactly one `ip: loopback:` log line and nothing given to a driver or to ARP; then it drains the ring, sends 65 and requires one more refusal, 193 counted, and still one line. `sh tests/host/ip_mutate.sh` logs every refusal, returns a refusal as 0, and drops the count, and each fails its row. Entry 226 numbered each datagram and added a row that the drain delivers the 64 the ring took, in the order sent, and none it refused, which the fault `overwrite` fails. The count is `loopback_dropped` in `/proc/stat`.
51. The probe suite cannot judge an installed machine (2026-09-12, milestone 50, found at the second sitting; severity: a proof not run, no fault in the kernel): the probes are written for the test image's boot, so on the installed root many fail for the environment. Three kinds: a hardcoded path, where `shelltest.c:136` execs a relative `hello` and `limitprobe.c:195` opens `/hello` before the exec of `/limitprobe` at `:206`; an expectation that is the boot root's own, where `dirprobe.c:212` requires a directory to list exactly its children, `unlinkdirprobe.c:247` requires every entry to be `DT_REG`, `mvtest.c:268` requires `rename` of a directory to be `EPERM`, and `vfsprobe.c:436` requires a link to be refused and `:445` requires KRFFS's own root magic; ext2 answers the first four as Linux does, returning its stored dot entries (`ext2.c:675`), hard links (`:2546`) and directory moves (`:2760`), and KRFFS's omission of `.` and `..` is deliberate (`sys/fs/Readme.md:15`) and permitted, since POSIX does not require entries that do not exist; and a fixture of the emulator's, where `dnstest.c:124` expects the emulator's addresses through the machine's real `/etc/hosts` (`utils/resolv.c:506`), `devprobe.c:198` requires stdin to be `/dev/console`, `orphanprobe` must be pid 1 to reap the orphan itself, and `modeprobe`'s children require an empty supplementary group list, which a session started by the SSH server does not have: `setresgid` leaves root's group 0 in the list, so a child that drops to uid 1000 is still in group 0 and the kernel is right to let it store setgid. That probe passes whole on the machine through a wrapper that calls `setgroups(0)` first. Of 53 probes run over SSH as root, 39 passed; the 14 failures were read as above, and each reading is a claim about that probe and not yet a rerun with its prerequisite supplied. Reproduce by copying `build/tests` onto an installed machine and running them. It leaves the machine's own acceptance to `metal_accept.sh` alone. Depends on nothing. Fixed when each probe states what it needs of its root or branches on the file system, and when a rerun on the machine with the fixtures supplied says what the kernel did and not where it ran. Fixed (2026-09-21): all ten named probes have explicit prerequisites in `tests/Readme.md`, and `vfsprobe` selects its hard-link and `statfs` checks from `/proc/mounts` and closes its openat directory before cleanup; `sh tests/system/probe_roots_mutate.sh` passes all ten on KRFFS and ext2, detects separately compiled ext2 link and magic faults with the expected assertion and exit status 1, and passes both roots after each restored, touched and rebuilt source produces the original kernel bytes. The ext2 and selfbuild-ext2 suites now run these ten probes on an ext2 root, the latter with the guest-built kernel. The local `tests/run default`, `tests/run ext2` and `tests/run selfbuild-ext2` commands were refused at socket preflight; the owner will run those integration checks on the testing host. The runs were made here on the testing host: `tests/run default ext2 selfbuild-ext2` passes, with the ten probes exiting 0 on the KRFFS root and in both ext2 runs, the second of them on the kernel the machine built. What is not claimed is a rerun on the installed notebook; the probes now say what they need of a root, which is what the entry asked for. This entry is closed. `tests/system/manual_probes.sh` is the script for that rerun: nine probes over SSH and `orphanprobe` at the console. It ran on the Lenovo on 2026-09-25 on the final kernel and root, and the nine passed over SSH, after entry 208's fault in the script was fixed. The owner waived the tenth, the console run of `orphanprobe`, the same day: it must be the init process, which only the kernel's debug shell at the console can make it, and what it tests, the kernel handing orphans to init, takes no device, while the default suite runs it in QEMU at every set on the same kernel binary as the machine's. The probes mode removed the nine it staged after their run, and the `clean` mode then removed `/orphanprobe`.
52. Every frame was copied into the card a byte at a time (2026-09-13, milestone 50, found at the second sitting; severity: a slow result, the link at a third of itself): `rtl8168.c:1189` moved the frame into the chip's transmit buffer with `for (i = 0; i < len; i++) buf[i] = data[i];`, and `e1000.c:705` did the same. The kernel is built at -O0, so that loop is a load, a store and a bound test for every byte. Timed on the machine with a cycle counter around the two halves of `ethernet_tx`, per 1514-byte frame: the frame's own assembly, three `k_memcpy` calls, cost 291 cycles, and `netdev_tx` cost 12330. That is about 6 us a frame, where a gigabit at full frames allows 12.3 us, and it is why the machine could not fill its link: UDP out ran at 73 MB/s and TCP at 57, both with the processor at 100 percent and 0.03 s of idle in 3.5 s. Reproduce with a sender of 1400-byte datagrams and a look at `/proc/stat`'s idle column. It breaks the Readme's claim of a gigabit interface in the only way that matters. Depends on nothing. Fixed in `ac48f68b`: both drivers move the frame with `k_memcpy` and pad the runt with `k_memset`, which are the kernel's word-at-a-time routines since entry 43. Measured on the machine after the change: UDP out 119 MB/s, which is the wire, TCP out 79, SSH out 29.8 against 26.2 and SSH in 27.9 against 19.7. The RTL8168 host test takes `kstring.c` in its dependencies and the e1000 host test has a copy of its own, both passing; the network suites in the emulator are unchanged. The same byte-at-a-time pattern was in TCP's four ring copies, `tcp.c:531` and `:281` for the send ring and `socket.c` for the receive ring, and is fixed the same way in `1d9c384e`, the ring's wrap making each copy at most two calls. With both taken, measured on the machine: TCP out 117 MB/s and TCP in 116, against UDP's 119 and a wire that carries 118. The link is full in both directions where it began at 57 out and 69 in. SSH carries 37.5 MB/s out and, after entry 54, 4.5 in, where a raw socket on the same machine carries 117 and 116. That deficit is entry 55 and its cause is not localized.

53. The ported e2fsprogs splits an unaligned buffer into block writes (2026-09-13, milestone 50, found at the second sitting; severity: unnecessary I/O, with its cost during the install still unproved). The port's `unix_io.c` sets `io->align = 512` and tests the caller's buffer address as well as the offset and length. A buffer allocated by `realloc` need not satisfy that alignment, so it takes the bounce path and one file-system block per call. Rach2's raw window requires sector-aligned offsets and lengths but copies the bytes into kernel memory itself. `vendor/e2fsprogs/patches/buffer-alignment.patch` therefore removes the address test and retains the offset, length and partial-sector checks. The original claim of 477659 write calls reduced to 9346 on a full-size grow, and its attribution of the install's ten minutes, are withdrawn. The controlled 1 GiB to 16 GiB grow costs 9595 disk commands with either binary, taking 8 seconds patched and 9 with the address test restored; the file-backed machine measurements likewise show no difference. The native tests of the port's actual I/O code prove the patch: 64 checks pass, 28 misaligned checks fail with the address test restored, and 64 pass after restoring the patch. Phase 6 records the cases and artifacts. It breaks no written contract. Depends on nothing. Closed in milestone 50 with the physical measurement on 2026-09-16: the recovery grow on the Samsung SSD issued 18751 disk commands and 9366 flushes in resize, against 4 commands and 1 flush for the table and 463 commands and no flush for the read-only check. The raw before/after samples are in `build/machine-50/sitting/machine-grow.caIiGM/grow.log`; entry 58 gives all four phases and the clock limit. The near one-to-two ratio records the cost of write-through I/O. It does not establish an unaligned-buffer split: `rach2/sys/block/ahci.c` counts the flush itself as a command and flushes each write, while this recovery port already removes the buffer-address check. These aggregate counters contain no buffer addresses or request lengths and no paired unpatched run. The patch's proof remains the native mutation; no physical speedup is claimed. The second physical grow with the committed recovery fixes reproduces every phase's command and flush counts exactly; its samples are in `build/machine-50/sitting2/machine-grow.ghNlAl/grow.log`.

54. The poller's original throughput claim was caused by the card fault (2026-09-13, milestone 50; attribution corrected in third-close unit D). The original report attributed a finite-timeout receiver's 6.4 MB/s, against 111.8 MB/s after a change, to leaving the wait queues before halting. That attribution is withdrawn: entry 55's card fault produced one receive window per tick. Those rates also lack an independent wall-clock check. The current order keeps the waiter registered through the halt, which is retained for clarity. On one processor the interrupt that wakes a halted process also ends its halt; the next scan finds the data even with the old queue order, so the requested lost-wake mutation could not demonstrate the claimed fault. Phase 6 records that source analysis and the old-order pipe result. The third close built distinct poll artifacts and recorded successful 256 MiB transfers, but the measurement logs do not identify the booted slot or connect it to the expected kernel. The reported installation and reboot sequence does not supply that missing per-run evidence. The two inbound groups read 2.26 to 2.29 s and 2.27 to 2.30 s in delivered guest ticks, which can be lost with interrupts masked. They establish neither a wall-time difference nor a comparison of known poll orders. The claim that these runs completed a kernel comparison is withdrawn. The program and artifact hashes remain in `notes.md`; no wrong-kernel run is alleged. This entry closes by correcting the unsupported attribution and retaining the source-supported order, without adding a machine comparison to the sitting. A future performance claim needs new boot provenance and independent timing.

55. SSH carried a twentieth of what a raw socket does inbound (2026-09-13, milestone 50, found at the second sitting; severity: a slow result on the path every update and every save uses). Fixed: the cause was the network card's message discipline, not Dropbear and not the cipher. The handler acknowledged the sources by writing back the bits it had read and returned with them unmasked; a message is an edge, so a handler that returns with any source still set is the last one the card ever runs. On the machine the status stood at 0x0081 after the acknowledgement, a frame received and the transmit ring empty, `/proc/interrupts` froze at the card's vector partway through the first large transfer, and every frame after that waited for the timer's pump, which takes one receive window a tick: 65536 bytes per 10 ms for a socket and Dropbear's 24576-byte channel window for SSH. Measured before the fix: a raw receiver read 64 MiB at 6.4 MB/s in exactly 100 reads a second of 64096 bytes; SSH inbound ran at 2.4 MB/s with the client sending 24648 bytes and pausing 9.87 ms, in a capture on the host; a 16 MiB inbound transfer cost the machine 728 interrupts over 6.85 s, of which 685 were the timer's. `rach2/sys/network/driver/rtl8168.c` now masks the sources for the length of the handler and unmasks at its end, which is the edge that carries whatever arrived meanwhile, and a storm leaves them masked. Measured after, with the kernel of this tree installed through `bootctl` and committed: SSH inbound 22.0 to 22.6 MB/s over three 48 MiB transfers, raw TCP inbound 118.3 MB/s, SSH outbound unchanged at 35.7. Proved by the row in `tests/host/rtl8168_hosttest.c` that models the chip's edge, which fails when the mask and the unmask are removed and passes when they are restored. Dropbear's receive window is not the limit and the port is unchanged.

56. The machine stopped answering while a probe wrote and unlinked in a loop on the installed ext2 root (2026-09-13, milestone 50, found while closing entry 51; severity: the machine stops answering and needs hands on it; nothing is known lost). What was run, as root over SSH on the installed machine: `/tmp/p2/fdstale`, the stale-descriptor probe, whose leak loops make 256 rounds of a file opened, filled with sixteen writes of 4096 bytes, unlinked and closed. The first run was still going after 20 seconds with nothing printed, its state R and `/fdstale.leak` open, which is the loop working and not a hang: 16 MiB in 4 KiB calls on a disk where such a call is four commands and two flushes is tens of seconds. That process was killed with SIGKILL while it held an unlinked file open, its leftovers were not all removed (`/fdstale.ddir` is a directory and the `rm` refused it), and the probe was started again at once. Within 60 seconds the machine stopped answering SSH, and it did not answer ICMP for the fifteen minutes after. The kernel it was running is this tree's, installed through `bootctl` and committed an hour before, and it had just carried three 48 MiB SSH transfers and a 64 MiB raw one without fault. What is not known is what the kernel did: the screen was not readable with the owner away, and the installed boot line carries no `rach2.netconsole`, so there is no record. Reproduce, in the emulator first, on an ext2 root: run the probe, kill it with SIGKILL in its leak loop while it holds an unlinked file, leave its leftovers in place, and run it again. It breaks the rule a user program cannot stop the machine, which entry 6 states and its fix made good for a process that stays inside system calls. Depends on nothing. One fault on that path is named and repaired. Its forced-wait proof and the reported machine load completed; the count is not a common-hour throughput result. The reading, which Astra made at the close and which was checked line by line here: a process killed by a signal is marked `PROC_ZOMBIE` at `kernel.c:398` before its descriptors are closed at `:418`; an ext2 close takes the mount's namespace lock (`ext2.c:359`), and a wait for that lock ends with the waiter `PROC_RUNNING` whatever it was before (`wait_queue.c:126`); `schedule` then makes a RUNNING process READY (`sched.c:109`), so the dying process is picked again, returns from its last `schedule`, and runs the bare `cli; hlt` that followed it, which no keyboard, timer or frame can end and which writes no record. That is a machine with its screen lit answering nothing, which is what was seen twice. The first repair made both teardowns assert the zombie again after their cleanup, and the halt is a panic that names the fault rather than a silent stop. `tests/zombieprobe.c` kills forty processes holding unlinked files while another writes the same file system, and runs in `tests/run default` and on an ext2 root in `tests/run ext2`; it passed with the fault compiled back in, so it is the stress and not the proof, and the repair rests on the reading. The third close replaces the separate teardowns with `proc_exit_cleanup`, publishing death only after cleanup. `exit_hosttest` drives the real wait queue with callbacks that force a contended close and working-directory release: five cases complete six waits. The fixed source passes, an early zombie store fails the publication and final-state assertions, and the restored source passes. On 2026-09-15 the installed notebook ran twenty SSH sessions, started a second apart, each copying sources before its own 3600-second guest-tick window on the reported `e1eec90c` installation: 400 successful build completions across those twenty sessions, including any final build finishing after its deadline, none failed, with fresh logins answering throughout and afterwards. The 114-record kernel log showed four waits for the request pool, with no panic, VM fault, quarantined request, stalled device or read-only remount. The installed boot line includes `rach2.netconsole`, and `/dev/bootlog` supplies the retained kernel log. No common measurement window or per-build finish times were recorded, and lost ticks can extend the wall duration. This records completed load with responsive logins; it does not establish 400 completions in one wall-clock hour.

57. A local connection turns over one window a tick (2026-09-13, milestone 50, found while measuring entry 50 on the machine; severity: a slow result on every local service). Measured on the installed machine: 256 MiB through the loopback was reported as 40.9 s, 6.0 MB/s using guest ticks without an independent wall-time check, and the receiver read exactly 100 times a second, 65536 bytes a read, whatever the sender's write size, at 4096, 16384 and 65536 bytes. The cause is the threshold the drain at a system call's exit uses. The loopback ring is delivered at that exit only once it holds three datagrams or more, since one or two may be the crossing SYNs of a simultaneous open, which `loopprobe` requires to work; a single datagram, which is what a window update or a bare acknowledgement is, therefore waits for the 100 Hz timer. A receiver that has drained its window sends one window update and then blocks, so the sender waits a tick for it, every window. Fixed: `idle_wait_irq_off` delivers what the ring holds before the processor is parked and returns without halting, so the caller checks its condition again. Those datagrams are the machine's own, a delivery is what makes a process runnable, and nothing else is going to run to deliver them; interrupts are already off, which is the discipline the drain needs, and a caller about to halt holds nothing, which is what the timer's drain relies on too. Proved by `loopprobe`'s round-trip row: two hundred one-byte round trips between two processes over the loopback take 20 ms, and 4020 ms with the drain removed from the idle path, which is one tick a leg. The earlier physical result was reported as 1.33 s and 201 MB/s for 256 MiB, against 40.9 s and 6.0 MB/s. These are guest-clock figures without an independent wall-time check or per-run kernel identity. It breaks no written contract. Depends on entry 50. The third close recorded eight more 256 MiB loopback transfers on 2026-09-15, with 4096- and 65536-byte writes and either no select timeout or ten seconds: 1.33 to 1.37 s each in delivered guest ticks, giving a calculated 196 to 202 MB/s. Masked interrupts can lose ticks and inflate those rates; they are not measured wall-time rates. Each finite-versus-infinite pair differed by at most one 10 ms tick. Across the 2 GiB, `loopback_dropped` stayed zero and `loopback_peak` stayed 47. The source at `e1eec90c` has 64 slots, but these logs do not identify the running kernel; attribution to that commit is withdrawn. The 114-record boot log still began with `boot: Rach2 Booting` and contained no loopback message. The functional fix remains supported by the guest mutation above; the physical byte counts and retained log support completion and entry 50's log condition. This entry makes no verified per-kernel throughput claim. Entry 54's claimed poll comparison is withdrawn, not completed by these runs.

58. The install's ten minutes were attributed to an unmeasured grow (2026-09-13, milestone 50, opened at the close when entry 53's measurement did not reproduce; severity: a slow result in the one step the owner waits for). The install of 2026-09-12 grew the root to 487106928 sectors and the whole run took ten minutes, which entry 53 attributed to the port's buffer-alignment test. That attribution does not survive measurement: the controlled grow of a 1 GiB volume to 16 GiB costs 9595 disk commands and 8 seconds with the port as it stands and 9595 commands and 9 seconds with the alignment test put back, and a file-backed grow on the machine costs the same either way. The required proof was a recovery grow of the machine's own geometry with separate table, check and resize measurements. It breaks no written contract. Depends on nothing. Closed by that measurement on 2026-09-16, on the Lenovo's Samsung SSD 870 EVO, serial `S6P2NX0T381638Z`, from the prepared recovery stick. Partition 3 grew from 2097152 to 488130928 sectors. The phases report respectively (microseconds, disk commands, flushes): table (10000, 4, 1), check-readonly (100000, 463, 0), check-repair (100000, 469, 2), resize (14540000, 18751, 9366). Their sum is 14.75 guest-tick seconds, of which resize is 14.54 seconds or 1454 ticks. CLOCK_MONOTONIC counts delivered 10 ms ticks and can lose ticks with interrupts masked; the short phases are one and ten ticks, not independently measured wall intervals. No independent host interval is present in the supplied phase log. This measures the grow, excluding seed copying, backup verification and other install work; it does not reconstruct the elapsed time of the earlier complete install. The ten-minute grow claim is withdrawn. The raw log is `build/machine-50/sitting/machine-grow.caIiGM/grow.log`. The original root was restored and checked clean afterwards, with the workaround and new faults recorded in entries 94 through 96. The second independent machine run with the committed fixes totals 14.81 guest-tick seconds, including 14.60 for resize, with identical command and flush counts in all four phases. The 0.06-second difference is six 10 ms ticks. Its log is `build/machine-50/sitting2/machine-grow.ghNlAl/grow.log`; these repeated readings retain the same clock limit.

59. A pulled stick leaves a disk in the registry and a mount that answers from the cache (2026-09-14, milestone 50, found while installing from the stick; severity: a wrong result, and a person told a volume is there when it is gone). On the recovery system booted from the install stick, the stick was pulled while its volume was mounted at `/mnt/install-source`. The kernel recorded the removal, `usb: device: port 3: gone`, `usbdisk: gone: device 2`, `usb: slot: 2: given back`, and yet `/proc/disk` still lists `disk 1 usb 0 120164352 mbr USB SanDisk 3.2Gen1` as `removable mounted`, `/proc/mounts` still carries `/dev/disk1p2 /mnt/install-source exfat rw`, and `ls` of that directory still prints the manifest and the image with their sizes. Only a read of the bytes says otherwise: `dd` returns nothing and `cat` of the manifest gives an I/O error. A person reading the listing is told the install source is there when it is not. Reproduce by mounting a stick's volume, pulling the stick, then listing the mount point and reading a file from it. It breaks what `/proc/disk` and `/proc/mounts` promise, which is what the machine has now, not what it had.
