Entry 226 replaced that with a stricter rule: each probe must end as its fault makes it end, a status or signal 11, and print its own row's failure, and `sigprobe`'s kill must come with the kernel's record of a fault at address zero. The script then passed all six of its rows on the testing host on 2026-09-25, after entry 212's change to its last.

211. No suite ran the harness's selftest (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found while recording entry 197's run; severity P3: rows that only a mutation script runs). `build/tests/system/harness --selftest` has the rows of entries 165 and 197, the late output among them, and only `harness_input_mutate.sh` and `krffs_inode_mutate.sh` ran it, so a full set passed whatever those rows said. Reproduce with `grep -n -- '--selftest' tests/system/suites.mk`, which finds no call of the harness. It leaves the rows out of every set, so a change that breaks them passes `tests/run all`. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: `tests/run runner` runs the harness's selftest before the runner's, and did in the full sets of 10:10, 14:49 and 15:24 by the testing host's clock on 2026-09-25.

212. `arping` without a reply, and `ifconfig` without an interface, never did what the manual listed (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the first run of `tests/system/rows_mutate.sh` to reach entry 175's row; severity P2: the timeouts of the staged programs never fire). The kernel registered no `setitimer`, so musl's `alarm` armed nothing, and every staged program that bounds a wait with `SIGALRM` waited until another event ended the wait, since the signal never came: BusyBox's `arping` paces its requests and ends a run by it, and sends one request and waits until interrupted, whatever `-c` and `-w` say; `login`, `ftpd`, `httpd`, `nc -w`, `wget -T` and `ntpd -q` bound their waits the same way, and `inetd` turns a service it disabled back on by it. On the Lenovo, `arping -f -w 5 192.168.0.253` was still waiting when `timeout` killed it at 20 s, and `pause` under `alarm(1)` never returned. And the kernel does not answer `SIOCGIFCONF`, so `ifconfig` fails with no interface named, with `-a`, and with a name `/proc/net/dev` does not list, printing `SIOCGIFCONF: Not a tty`. Reproduce with those commands on any image. `busybox-networking(1)` listed both tools with nothing said, which breaks the manual's contract that a page says where the code differs from its upstream. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, as the owner chose on 2026-09-25: the kernel keeps a real-time interval timer, `setitimer(ITIMER_REAL)`, `getitimer` and `alarm` (`setitimer(2)`), whose `SIGALRM` the timer tick posts (`proc_wake_sleepers` in `rach2/sys/proc/process.c`); `sigprobe` has its rows, which `tests/system/itimer_mutate.sh` failed with the tick's post taken out and with the rearm taken out, and since entry 215 with the check of `which` taken out and with the blocked mask taken out of `signal_pending`, the restored kernels passing and matching the first, and the dhcp suite requires `arping -w 2` at an address nothing answers to end with status 1 and no reply, which it did; on the Lenovo the same run ended after three requests. `SA_RESTART` is still not honored (`sigaction(2)`), so `arping` prints `arping: recvfrom: Interrupted system call` for each alarm that interrupts its wait but the one that ends the run, and `busybox-networking(1)` says so. The virtual and profiling timers are refused, since the kernel keeps no processor time per process. `ifconfig`'s limit is the manual's to say, as it says of `ftpd` and `chroot`, and the page says it. `rows_mutate.sh` gives entry 175's fault a missing interface, which fails at once.

213. `stattest`'s time row judged the host's load (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by `tests/run default` in the close's run of 09:05 by the testing host's clock; severity P3: a false failure, no fault in the kernel). The row required fifty stamps taken back to back to be strictly increasing. `rtc_realtime` in `rach2/sys/rtc.c` never gives a time earlier than its last: a reading that comes out earlier, which the emulator can give when the tick's interrupt reaches the PIC after the counter has wrapped, is replaced by the last time again. So two stamps can be equal, and the row failed with `STATTIME FAIL: two writes, fifty stamps, explicit fractions` in a run beside four other suites, having passed in every run before it that day. Reproduce by running the default suite under load until it fails. It breaks the rule that a suite does not judge the clock. It depends on entry 166. Fixed here: the stamps must never go back, and one of them must have a part below the 10 ms tick of at least a microsecond, which a clock of whole ticks never gives and a repeated reading does not take away; `rtc_mutate.sh --guest`'s coarse clock failed that on the testing host on 2026-09-25, and the restored clock passed.

214. A sync after an edit made during a run left the testing host's objects newer than their sources (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found when the timer's rows failed on a kernel whose `syscall.o` had not been rebuilt; severity P2: a run can judge a kernel built from sources it was not given, and from objects built from two versions of one header). `tools/dev/testing.sh` synced with `rsync -a`, which gives each file the time it was edited here. `rach2/sys/syscall.c` was edited at 09:52 by the testing host's clock while a run there built `syscall.o` at 09:55 from the older text; the next sync delivered the new text with the time 09:52, and make took the object of 09:55 for up to date. `rach2/sys/proc/process.h` changed the same way, so the objects built during that run from the old header and `process.o`, built after it, disagreed on the size of the process record. The run's kernel logged `syscall: 104: not implemented`. Reproduce by editing a source here while a run on the host rebuilds its object, and syncing afterwards. It breaks the rule that a run judges the tree it was given. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: the sync compares by content and gives a file that differs the time it lands, `--no-times --checksum`, which the form in `CLAUDE.md` now has too; the run that met it was stopped, and the host's `build/` was removed, its vendor outputs kept, before the next build. That build made the kernel `273bd0bf`, and the full set on it passed 89 of 90 suites, `style` failing on three findings of the close's own changes, which are fixed and which it then passed alone.

215. The interval timer's first alarm could come up to a tick early, and two of its rows could not fail as written (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by a scan of the day's changes that the session ran with three reviewers after the owner asked whether the work had converged; severity P3: an alarm set for 10 ms could arrive at once, and a refusal and a single alarm were judged by rows that passed with their fault in the kernel or failed with no fault in it). `itimer_set` in `rach2/sys/syscall.c` armed the first `SIGALRM` at the tick `value` ticks after the current count, and part of the current tick has passed when the call runs, so the alarm came between `value - 1` and `value` ticks later; `getitimer` reported the whole count, a tick more than the lower end of the time left. `sigprobe`'s row for the virtual timer passed a time of a million microseconds, which the kernel refuses whatever the timer, so the row passed with the check of `which` removed; and its single alarm had to end a five-second sleep begun after the alarm was armed, so a host that ran the probe late could deliver the alarm before the sleep began and fail the row with no fault in the kernel. `itimer_mutate.sh` set its mark that the source had changed only after writing the source, so a signal between the two left the fault in the tree. And `setitimer(2)` gave musl's `alarm` the raw call's rounding to the nearest second, where musl rounds any fraction up, and did not say that a null `new` is refused where Linux disarms the timer, or that `execve` clears a `SIGALRM` still pending. Reproduce the first by arming a timer of one tick and reading `kernel_ticks` when its signal arrives. It breaks `setitimer(2)`'s contract that the time asked for is a minimum, and the rule that a row fails when its fault is present. It depends on entry 212. Fixed here: the first alarm is armed a tick later, since the tick in progress is not counted, and `getitimer` reports the time left less that tick, one tick at least while the timer is armed, so a timer read in the tick it was armed reads back as the time set, rounded up to whole ticks and limited to the clamp, or a tick more where its due tick would be zero. A review of that fix found that the longest timer, armed where its tick wrapped past zero, read back as one tick, since `proc_alarm_at` moves a tick of zero to one; the timer's ticks are clamped at 0x7FFFFFFD, a tick below the sleep's. `tests/host/syscall_hosttest.c` requires a timer of three ticks armed at tick 1000 to be due at 1004 and to read back as three ticks, and the longest timer armed at tick 0x80000001 to read back as the longest, and `syscall_mutate.sh`'s faults `timer-first`, `timer-left` and `timer-clamp` fail those rows. `sigprobe` refuses the virtual timer with a valid time and requires it to arm nothing; arms its single alarm with `SIGALRM` blocked and requires the alarm not to end a one-second sleep and to leave the timer disarmed once it fires, and its handler to run once when the signal is unblocked; replaces an alarm an hour away in its `alarm` row, where an alarm of five seconds could fire first on a late host; and leaves the timer disarmed after every row. `itimer_mutate.sh` sets its mark before it writes, changes `syscall.c` and `syscall_proc.c` as well as `process.c`, and has two faults more: the check of `which` removed, which fails the virtual timer's row, and the blocked mask taken out of `signal_pending`, which fails the blocked alarm's row. The review of the fix found that the single alarm's count of one could not fail, since posts of a blocked signal merge into one, that the blocked alarm's row had no fault to prove it, and that the `alarm` row still judged the clock. On the testing host's build from clean of 15:08 on 2026-09-25, `itimer_mutate.sh` failed each of its four rows with its fault, each restored kernel matching the first, and `syscall_mutate.sh` failed each timer row with its fault. `setitimer(2)` now says how musl's `alarm` rounds, that a null `new` is refused where Linux disarms the timer, and that `execve` clears a pending `SIGALRM`. A second review, of those fixes, found that a timer read with `getitimer` in its last tick and passed straight back came due a tick later each time, so a program that did that at every tick was never signalled; that no row could see the tick's rearm, or its post to a process that was not asleep; that no row could see a timer of under a tick rounded down to no tick; and that two rows of entry 134 could not show their faults (entry 221). The session first answered the drift by reading from the timer chip where in its tick each call came, and arming the first alarm at the first tick at or after the time asked for. A third review found that under QEMU the timer's interrupt is raised a millisecond or two after its period begins, so that reading could be a tick behind and the alarm come up to a tick early, and the change was withdrawn: the timer counts whole ticks, and `setitimer(2)` gives the drift as a limit. `tests/host/process_timer_hosttest.c` compiles `proc_wake_sleepers` and `proc_alarm_at` from `process.c`, with a blocked process ahead of a running one, each with a timer, and requires a due timer to post `SIGALRM` to its process, running or blocked, and be armed again for its interval from that tick; a single alarm to post once and be left disarmed; a sleeper to be made runnable at its deadline and not before; a blocked process with no deadline and a running process to be left alone; and a wrapped alarm tick to be moved to 1. The third review found that a list of one running process let a post to the wrong process pass, and that each boundary had a fault on one side only; a fourth found no row for a tick that both wakes a sleeper and posts an alarm, for an interval of one tick, or for the count's wrap, and the test has rows for each. `deadline_mutate.sh` has seventeen faults for these rows. `syscall_hosttest.c` has rows for a timer of 5 ms, which must be armed; an interval of 5 ms, which must round up to a tick; a timer of 1 s, which must read back as 1 s; seconds past the clamp, which must shorten the timer rather than wrap it; and `alarm`'s rounding to the nearest second, and to 1 under a second. The fourth review found that no row checked an interval of a second or more, the longest timer to the tick, `alarm`'s half second or the interval it drops, the mask the timer is armed under, or the refusals of a whole second of microseconds and of a timer other than `ITIMER_REAL`, and the test has rows for each. A fifth found that no row armed a timer of under half a tick, so rounding a time to the nearest tick passed, and that the row `and alarm(0) disarms it` did not look at the timer; the test has a row for a timer of 1 us, and the row requires the timer disarmed. `syscall_mutate.sh` has twenty-one faults for the timer. `setitimer(2)` says that its errors are the raw call's, since musl's wrappers read and write the structures themselves, and that Linux arms a periodic timer again only when its signal is delivered.

216. A sleep, and a `poll` or `select` timeout, could end up to a tick early (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the scan of entry 215; severity P3: a program that sleeps 10 ms could be woken at once, which POSIX does not allow for `nanosleep` or for the timeouts of `poll` and `select`). `sys_nanosleep` in `rach2/sys/syscall.c`, and `ms_deadline` and `ts_deadline` in `rach2/sys/io/poll.c`, set the deadline to the current count plus the ticks asked for, and the tick in progress has partly passed, so the wait lasted between a tick less than the time asked and that time. Reproduce by reading `kernel_ticks` around a `nanosleep` of 10 ms started just before a tick. It breaks the contract of `nanosleep(2)`, `poll(2)` and `select(2)` that the interval is rounded up. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: each deadline adds the tick in progress, so a nonzero wait that neither a signal nor a ready descriptor ends first is never shorter than asked, up to the clamp; the tick counts are clamped one lower, at 2147483646, so the signed difference stays valid with that tick added; and `nanosleep`'s remainder after a signal leaves the added tick out, so a caller that sleeps again for the remainder does not gain a tick at each signal. A review of that fix found two faults of `nanosleep` more. A signal in the tick before the deadline left a remainder of zero, so a caller that slept again for it ended early, and the fix made it leave one tick. And a deadline that wrapped to 0 was moved to 1 before the loop compared it, so the longest sleep begun at tick 0x80000001 returned at once and a sleep of zero at tick 0 blocked for a tick; the loop now compares the deadline as computed, and only its store in `wakeup_tick` is moved off zero, as `poll`'s already was. `tests/host/poll_deadline_hosttest.c` compiles `poll.c` with its system call entries made private and requires `poll` and `ppoll` timeouts of 30 ms from tick 1000 to end at 1004, and `tests/host/deadline_mutate.sh` takes the added tick out, which fails those rows, and raises the `ppoll` clamp a tick, which fails the row on the longest timeout. On the testing host's build from clean of 15:08 on 2026-09-25, `syscall_mutate.sh`'s faults for this entry, `sleep-deadline`, `sleep-remainder`, `sleep-last-tick` and `sleep-wrap`, failed the rows on the three-tick block, the whole remainder, the tick then left in the last tick and the wrapped deadline, and `deadline_mutate.sh` failed its rows with the added tick taken out and with the clamp raised. A second review, of those fixes, found that a sleep resumed for its remainder never ended when a signal came at every tick, since the tick of remainder the last tick left and the tick each resumed sleep added kept its deadline ahead; that a signal at the deadline ended the sleep with `EINTR` and a zero remainder where Linux returns 0; that the idle after a yield that ran nothing could lose a tick (entry 220); and that four faults that shorten a `poll`, `ppoll` or `select` timeout passed `poll_deadline_hosttest.c`. The session's reading of the timer chip, which entry 215 describes, was tried for the sleep as well and withdrawn with it. `nanosleep` now tests its deadline before its signals, so once the deadline tick has come it returns 0 whatever is pending; a signal in the last tick again leaves no remainder, so a sleep resumed under a signal at every tick ends, at most a tick short of the first sleep's deadline, which `nanosleep(2)` gives as a limit; and the sleep halts with interrupts off (entry 220). `syscall_hosttest.c` requires a sleep of three ticks from tick 1000 to block until 1004 and return there; a signal already pending to leave the whole request as the remainder; a signal in the last tick to leave none; a signal at the deadline to end nothing; a sleep resumed under a signal at every tick to end within four calls; a remainder of 199 ticks to read as 1 s and 990 ms; a wake by a signal the sleep does not act on to send it back to sleep with its deadline armed again; the longest sleep from tick 0x80000000 to block; a sleep of 42949673 s to be shortened rather than wrapped; a deadline that wraps to 0 to be stored as 1; a sleep of zero not to block; and each halt to run masked. The third review found that the model's clock could run backward, and that no row had a remainder of a second or more, a wake with nothing pending or the halt's mask; the model's clock now moves forward only. The fourth review found that no row rounded a sleep's nanoseconds, looked at the mask or the process after the call, saw the zero remainder written on completion, the deadline stored when the mask is lifted, a sleeper switched back to, a deadline tick that comes as the sleep masks after a yield or after a halt, the count of a sleep cut short in its last tick, or the checks of the request; and that its fault `sleep-idle-on` changed nothing the kernel depends on, since a halt entered with the mask on is as safe in either form. The model now lets the deadline's tick in just before the sleep masks, switches back to the sleeper, ends a halt on another interrupt and marks the remainder before each call, `sleep-halt-unmasked` replaces that fault, and the test has a row for each. The fourth review also found that a branch of `sys_nanosleep` that gave a nonzero request one tick could never run, since the nanoseconds already round up, and it is removed. A fifth review found that with the one-tick branch gone no row asked for a nonzero time under a tick, so rounding to the nearest tick, or one short of a tick, passed, and that no row gave a negative nanosecond count; the test has rows for a sleep of 1 ns and of -1 ns. `syscall_mutate.sh` has twenty-eight faults for the sleep, one or more for each of those rows. `poll_deadline_hosttest.c` has rows for a `ppoll` and a `select` of 1 s and 11 ms, a `select` of 1 us and of zero, the longest `ppoll` timeout to the tick and across the wrap, and the deadline's comparison, which `do_poll` now makes through `deadline_reached`; the fourth review found that a `ppoll` of 15 ms could not tell rounding up from rounding to the nearest tick, and that a `select`'s seconds, a zero `select` and the wrap had no row. The fifth review found the same gap in `ppoll`'s nanoseconds, where rounding one short of a tick passed every row; the test has a row for a `ppoll` of 1 ns, and one for a `poll` of 1 ms beside it. `deadline_mutate.sh` has fourteen faults for them, among them the comparison moved a tick either way and made unsigned.

217. A wait on a wait queue lost its deadline to a signal it does not end on (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the scan of entry 215; severity P3: the kernel's `ping` to an address that does not answer could wait until a person interrupted it). `signal_post` clears `wakeup_tick` when it wakes a blocked process, whether the signal is caught, blocked or ignored. `wq_wait` in `rach2/sys/proc/wait_queue.c` then found no signal to act on, blocked again, and had no timer to end the wait. The one caller that arms a deadline before such a wait is the ping of `rach2/sys/network/ip/icmp.c`, whose wait ends on a reply or at its deadline. Reproduce by resizing the terminal of a `ping` to an address that does not answer: the terminal's `SIGWINCH`, ignored by default, wakes the wait, and the ping then waits until a reply comes or a signal it acts on, such as the terminal's interrupt, ends it. It breaks the ping's contract that each request waits at most its timeout. It depends on nothing, though entry 212 added a signal the kernel posts on its own. Fixed here: `wq_wait` reads the caller's deadline once and arms it again before each block; `tests/host/wait_queue_hosttest.c` has a model scheduler that wakes the waiter as `signal_post` does, and requires a wait whose condition is false twice to block twice with the deadline armed each time, and `tests/host/deadline_mutate.sh` removes the line that arms it again, which fails that row, as it did on the testing host's build from clean of 15:08 on 2026-09-25. The comments of `icmp.c`, which gave the timer's disarming as the reason the deadline is in the ping's predicate, and the contract of `wq_wait_event` in `wait_queue.h` say what the wait does now.

218. Two rejection rows of the harness's selftest judged the host's speed (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the scan of entry 215; severity P3: a false failure, no fault in the harness). `prompt_wait_selftest` in `tests/system/runner/harness.c` has a child write the prompt a second after the start, and the two cases that must reject it rely on the wait ending first, after a cap of 0.05 s and one read slice of 0.3 s, so a host that runs the harness most of a second late reads the prompt and fails the row. Reproduce by running `build/tests/system/harness --selftest` on a host loaded until a process waits a second for a processor. It breaks the rule that a suite does not judge the clock. It depends on entry 211, which put the selftest in `tests/run runner`. Fixed here: in the cases that must reject it the prompt comes five seconds after the start, well past the cap and the read slice, and a second after, or at once when the child has exited, in the cases that must read it; a rejected wait ends at once and its child is then killed, so the selftest takes no longer. `tests/run runner` passed with it in the full sets of 14:49 and 15:24 by the testing host's clock on 2026-09-25. A second review found that the five seconds let a cap or read slice under five seconds pass the rows. The comment now says so, and says that a step that waits on the run's timeout of ten seconds still fails them.

219. Six passages of the manual and the Readmes, and five comments, did not say what the code does (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the scan of entry 215; severity P3: documents that break the contract of stating what the code does). `syscalls(2)` said the kernel registers 120 calls, where entry 212 made it 123; `busybox-networking(1)` had a sentence on `ftpd` that the proofreading of `4c5aedf2` left garbled, and said `arping` reports every alarm that interrupts its wait, where the alarm that ends the run ends it without a message; `tests/system/Readme.md` gave `manual_probes.sh`'s `pipe` mode as a read of `/dev/disk0`, which entry 204 changed to the root's own partition; `tests/host/Readme.md` said each FAT32 sequence runs on a fresh volume and is read back, where the sequences run in turn on one volume and two of them were not read back (entry 209), and its list of the sequences left out two; and `rach2/sys/proc/signal.Readme.md` listed `sigprobe`'s rows without the one-shot handler, the sleep a signal ends and the timer. And five comments: `proc_wake_sleepers` in `process.h` said nothing of the timer's `SIGALRM`; `get_current_time` in `syscall_fs.c` gave the tick as the clock's resolution, which entry 166 made the PIT's; `utils/init.c` and `tests/system/suites/tcc.sh` said the kernel has no `setitimer`; and `tests/system/suites/selfbuild.sh` said the host's kernel has debug data, which entry 205 took out. Reproduce by reading each against its source. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, in `d09494bf` for the two pages and the system tests' Readme, `2b23da7a` for the host tests' Readme, `b4715459` for the signal Readme and `8c3451e8` for the comments: each passage states what the code does. A second review found more of the same kind: `setitimer(2)` gave the raw call's errors as the library's, where musl's wrappers fault in the caller, and left out that Linux arms a periodic timer again only when its signal is delivered; its claims, and those of `nanosleep(2)`, `select(2)` and `rach2/sys/io/Readme.md`, had no limit beside them; `busybox-networking(1)` quoted `arping`'s message without its prefix and left out `ifconfig`'s broadcast rule and the settings the kernel does not answer; `signal.Readme.md` placed the timer's calls in `syscall_proc.c`; three pages kept an older date; and the corrected `selfbuild.sh` comment gave the machine-built kernel as near 300 KB, where the selfbuild suite's log gives 1147548 bytes. A third review found that `proc(5)` did not say that only `nanosleep`'s sleeps are counted; that `nanosleep(2)`, `setitimer(2)` and `signal.Readme.md` gave `EINTR` for any pending signal, where a blocked or ignored one ends nothing; that `nanosleep(2)` left out that a stop and continue ends the sleep with `EINTR` where Linux restarts it, and `setitimer(2)` that Linux's `getitimer` still reports the interval; that `setitimer(2)` gave a null `old` as a fault in the caller; and that the `ifconfig` passage gave the flags wrongly and left out the broadcast an interface with no address accepts, `broadcast +`, and what `add` and `del` do. A fourth review found that `broadcast +` needs an address and a netmask earlier on the same command line and that the order of the requests matters; that `add` does not bring the interface up; that `signal.Readme.md` gave the deadline rule to every sleep and let a blocked signal end a wait; that `nanosleep(2)` spoke of the tick a request owns, said a pending signal is acted on as the call returns, where a blocked one stays pending, and gave Linux's difference in part; and that `setitimer(2)`, `poll(2)`, `sigaction(2)` and `rach2/sys/io/Readme.md` each left out a limit, a cross-reference or a Linux difference. A fifth review found that the `ifconfig` passage gave every address set alone the netmask of its class, where the address in force keeps its netmask and a class's network or broadcast address leaves none; that `nanosleep(2)` added the tick in progress to a zero interval too; that `poll(2)`, `select(2)` and `rach2/sys/io/Readme.md` called a signal deliverable where an ignored one ends nothing; that `sigaction(2)` left out that a blocked signal interrupts nothing and gave a stop's result as `EINTR` alone; and that `signal.Readme.md` gave an incomplete list of the waits a signal does not end, and made the alarm's result depend on when it is posted rather than on when the process runs. A sixth review found that `signal.Readme.md` gave `nanosleep`'s rule for the alarm to `poll` and `select`, which test the signal before their deadline, and still listed the waits a signal does not end in part; that `nanosleep(2)` still gave a stop's result as `EINTR`; that the `ifconfig` passage stated two narrow cases wrongly, since `broadcast +` takes the address and the netmask in either order and an address in force with no netmask takes its class's; and that `wait(2)`, `connect(2)` and the comments of `signal_pending` in `process.h` and `syscall_proc.c` called a signal deliverable where an ignored one ends nothing. A seventh review found that the `ifconfig` passage, `ioctl(2)` and `rach2/sys/network/Readme.md` left out that a netmask the kernel refuses while an address has its class's netmask, or while it waits for one, leaves the interface with no address, and that `nanosleep(2)` measured the remainder from the call's return, where after a stop the call returns when the process is continued and the remainder was stored when the signal ended the sleep. The Codex review of 2026-09-26 found fourteen passages more in the pages and Readmes the close changed, and its later rounds six, among them that `ifconfig -a` with an interface named works, that a timeout never expires early where the wait may still end early, that the time limits apply to the seconds first, that a failed netmask request unconfigures the interface only for `EINVAL`, and that the KRFFS write rule, the stager's identifier check, the recovery key reader and `disk write` were each described wrongly in part ([codex-2026-09-26.md](../06-review/codex-2026-09-26.md)). Each now states what the code does.

220. `nanosleep`'s idle could lose a tick (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the second review of entry 216; severity P3: a sleep could end a tick late, and a signal be acted on a tick late). After a yield that found nothing else to run, `sys_nanosleep` tested its deadline with interrupts on and then called `idle_wait_irq_on`, `sti; hlt`; a tick that came between the test and the halt was spent before the halt, which then waited for the next interrupt. Reproduce by reading the code; the window is a few instructions wide. It breaks `nanosleep(2)`'s deadline by a tick, never shortening it. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: the sleep reads its state with interrupts off after the yield and halts with `idle_wait_irq_off`, as `wq_wait` and `do_poll` do, so a tick that comes between that read and the halt ends the halt instead of passing before it. `syscall_hosttest.c`'s row `the sleep halts with interrupts masked` records the mask at each halt, and `syscall_mutate.sh`'s fault `sleep-halt-unmasked` puts the unmasked halt back; after the fourth review of entry 216 the host test has rows for a deadline tick that comes as the sleep masks after a yield or after a halt, which the faults `sleep-halt-read` and `sleep-left-early` fail.

221. Two rows of entry 134 could not show their faults (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the second review of entry 215; severity P3: a row that passed with its fault present, and one that could fail with none). `syscall_hosttest.c`'s row that the mask is lifted with the sleeper already blocked recorded only whether the yield ran masked, so moving `kirq_restore` above `current_proc->state = PROC_BLOCKED`, which reopens entry 134's lost wakeup, passed it. And `sigprobe`'s row 6 signalled its child a tenth of a second after the fork, taking it to be asleep by then, so a host that ran the child late could let the signal land before the sleep, and the child then slept its whole 30 s and failed the row with no fault in the kernel. Reproduce the first by making that swap in a copy of `syscall.c` and running the host test, the second by reading. It breaks the rule that a row fails when its fault is present, and only then. It depends on entry 134. Fixed here: the model's `kirq_restore` records the process's state whenever it lifts the mask, the row requires it blocked at the lift before the yield, and `syscall_mutate.sh`'s `sleep-block-order` fault makes that swap; and row 6 waits until `/proc/<pid>/stat` shows the child asleep before it signals, for at most 500 sleeps of 10 ms, and fails a row of its own if the child is never seen asleep.

222. Under the Mac's shell, a script with `set -e`, `set -u` and an EXIT trap reported success when an unset name stopped it (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the fifth review, the fourth of the rounds the owner asked for, reading entry 208; severity P2: a driver the Mac runs could report a pass for work it never did, as entry 208's did). Bash 3.2.57, which is `/bin/sh` and `/bin/bash` on the Mac, hands an EXIT trap a status of 0 when `set -u` stops a script that also has `set -e`, and a trap that ends in success lets the script exit 0; with `set -u` alone it exits 1, and the testing host's `dash` and bash 5.3 and Rach2's BusyBox `ash` each exit with a failing status. Entry 208 gave the cause as bash's POSIX mode, and said that no other script the Mac runs combines `set -u` with an EXIT trap. 117 of the tree's scripts combine `set -eu` with an EXIT trap, among them `tools/dev/machine_net_in.sh`, which the Mac runs against the Lenovo and which exited 0 with an unset name placed after its traps. Reproduce on the Mac by running `/bin/sh` on a script of `set -eu`, `trap : EXIT` and `echo "$unset"`, which exits 0. It breaks every such script's contract that status 0 means it finished. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: the eight scripts under `tools/` and `krffs/` that combine the three, seven of them drivers, build steps and reports a person runs on the Mac and `tools/dev/machine_grow.sh`, which the machine's recovery system runs under BusyBox `ash`, and `tests/system/accept/su_command.sh`, which `metal_accept.sh` runs from the Mac for the owner's `su` commands, keep a `finished` flag that they set where they succeed, and their EXIT trap turns a status of 0 into 1 when the flag is unset, as `manual_probes.sh`'s has since entry 208. `tools/verify/check.sh`'s conventions step refuses a script under `tools/` or `krffs/`, or `manual_probes.sh` or `su_command.sh`, that sets `-e` and `-u` with a `set` at the start of a line, after any other options, as `-eu` or `-Ceu`, in separate words or on separate lines, or as `-o errexit` and `-o nounset`, and traps EXIT or 0 with a `trap` at the start of a line, whose action may span lines, without the flag; `tools/Readme.md` lists the rule. The sixth review found `su_command.sh`, and that the rule matched only some of those spellings. The seventh found that the patterns widened for the sixth reported a script with no EXIT trap whose line ended in a quote and a 0, and a `set --` whose words had an `-e` or a `-u`, and still missed `set -Ceu` and a trap over several lines whose last line named other signals with EXIT; the patterns now take only option words before the flags, and a trap over several lines only where a `trap` line leaves its quote open. The other scripts with the pattern are suites, host tests and mutation tests, which run on the testing host under `dash`, where the status is right. On the Mac, `tools/verify/probecheck.sh` with an unset name placed after its traps exited 0 before the fix and 1 after, and the fixed script passes the tree with status 0; `su_command.sh`, with its two tools stubbed and an unset name after its traps, exited 0 before and 1 after. A run of the rule by hand, not a test in the tree, reported copies of `su_command.sh` and `tools/dev/poll_kernels.sh` with the flag's test taken out, the second with `set -e` and `set -u` on separate lines, a script whose trap spans two lines, and scripts with `set -e -u`, `set -o errexit -o nounset`, two traps on one line and a double-quoted trap over two lines; it passed two guarded scripts and reported nothing on the tree. After the seventh review's fix the rule, run alone under the Mac's bash 3.2 and under dash over those two copies and thirty-one planted scripts, reported the copies and nineteen scripts that combine the flags with an EXIT trap, among them `set -Ceu`, `set -euo pipefail` and a trap whose last line ends `' INT TERM EXIT`; passed the guarded script and five that end a line with a quote and a 0, run `set --` with an `-e` or a `-u` word, or set `-u` alone; and reported nothing on the tree. It misses `set -e; set -u` on one line, `#!/bin/sh -e`, a trap after `&&` and a trap continued with a backslash, and it reports a `set -e` inside a here-document and a `trap - EXIT`; the scope has none of these.

223. The console's login prompt timed out every minute once the interval timer worked (2026-09-25, milestone 06's close, found by the owner at the Lenovo's console after the day's fourth update; severity P3: an idle console redrew its prompt under `Login timed out after 60 seconds` every minute). BusyBox's `login` arms `alarm(60)` before it prints its prompt, and when nobody types it prints that line and exits; `init` then starts the next `login`. Before entry 212 the kernel had no `alarm`, so the prompt waited without a limit and the timeout never fired. Reproduce by leaving the metal image's console at its prompt for a minute. `login(1)` gives the 60-second default, so no page was wrong, but a console that repeats itself every minute is not one a person can read. It depends on entry 212. Fixed here, at the owner's word: `init` sets `LOGIN_TIMEOUT=0` in the console session's child before it starts `/bin/login`, so the console prompt waits without a limit, and `init(8)` and `login(1)` say so; a login started any other way reads the variable from the environment it is given. `tests/host/initpolicy_hosttest.c` runs `init`'s `spawn_shell` with a model exec that reports whether the first `LOGIN_TIMEOUT` of the environment it is given is `LOGIN_TIMEOUT=0`, once with the variable unset in the test's own environment and once with it set to 60, and then requires the test's own environment to be as it was. `tests/host/account_mutate.sh` has three faults for the row: `init-login-timeout` takes the setting out, `init-login-keep` lets an inherited value stand, and `init-login-parent` sets the variable in `init` before the fork rather than in the child. The seventh review, the first to read this fix, found that the row ran only with the variable unset and did not look at `init`'s own environment, so the last two faults passed it. On the testing host's build from clean of 23:32 on 2026-09-25, `account_mutate.sh` failed each of its twenty-two faults with its named row and passed each restored source.

224. The installer's copy of a recovery key could be made to overwrite a file of root's, a key of exactly 16384 bytes was written a byte longer than init reads, and a recovery partition mounted elsewhere was taken for none (2026-09-26, milestone 06's close, found by the Codex review of the fixes after the closing review, [codex-2026-09-26.md](../06-review/codex-2026-09-26.md); severity P1 for the first: a process that made `/tmp/rach2-install-key.PID` first, as a link to a file of root's, had the installer write over that file as root; P3 for the second; P2 for the third: an update then wrote the root with no archive of the machine's configuration behind it). `utils/rach2-install` copied the key it was offered to that fixed name, and judged the copy's size before `printf '%s\n'` added a newline to a last line that had none. And `find_recovery` ran in a command substitution, so its `die` ended only the subshell, and a partition it could not mount because it was mounted elsewhere, as init's recovery key reader leaves the recovery partition when its unmount fails, was passed over as if it were some other partition. Reproduce the first by making the name a link before the key prompt, the second with a key file of 16384 bytes and no final newline, the third by reading the code. It breaks the installer's contract that the key it places is the key it judged, and that an update keeps the machine's configuration archived until the new root is tried. It depends on nothing. Fixed here: the copy is made in a directory `mktemp -d` makes for the run, mode 0700, which the run removes as it ends; the copy's last line is ended before it is judged, and the copy is what is written; and `find_recovery` sets its answer in the main shell, and a partition that will not mount here but is mounted elsewhere stops the run with where it is mounted. On the Mac, under bash 3.2 and dash, the copy's steps taken from the script accepted keys of 87 and 88 bytes as 88 bytes, refused one of 16384 bytes with no final newline and accepted one of 16384 with it, and made the directory mode 0700, and `mounted_elsewhere`, over a sample table, named a foreign mount and passed over the run's own two. On the testing host's build from clean of 02:26 on 2026-09-26, `recovery_disk_key_mutate.sh`, whose installer fault follows the new copy, failed row 6b with that fault and passed its restoration, and the installer suite passed in the full set; the machine's seventh update ran the new installer from the new recovery system.

225. `dhcpc` could record an address the interface already had as its own to take off, when it wrote a lost claim again (2026-09-26, milestone 06's close, found by the Codex review of the fixes after the closing review, [codex-2026-09-26.md](../06-review/codex-2026-09-26.md); severity P3: a later `dhcpc -k` could take off an address the machine had before its lease). `claim_is_ours` in `utils/dhcpc.c` wrote a claim that had gone again with the address it holds even when `adopted` said the interface had that address before the lease, where the first lease's claim and a child's handoff record none (entry 198). Reproduce by removing the claim of a client that adopted its address and letting a renewal write it again. It breaks entry 198's contract that `dhcpc` takes off only an address it put on. It depends on entry 198. Fixed here: a claim written again records `adopted ? 0 : holding`. `tests/host/dhcpc_hosttest.c`'s row `check_claim_rewritten` writes a claim again with the address adopted and with it not, and `tests/host/netutil_mutate.sh`'s fault `claim-rewrite` fails it; on the Mac the test passed, the fault failed its row and the restored source passed, and on the testing host's build from clean of 02:26 on 2026-09-26 `netutil_mutate.sh` passed the same way.

226. Rows and mutation tests the close added or changed could pass with their faults or fail for another reason, and two tools could leave a command running or fail to build (2026-09-26, milestone 06's close, found by the Codex review of the fixes after the closing review, [codex-2026-09-26.md](../06-review/codex-2026-09-26.md), which gives each; severity P3: proofs that did not show what they claimed, and cleanups that could run on while a source was put back). The review found them in `tests/host/fat_barriers_mutate.sh`, which took any nonzero exit for a proved barrier and never ran a restored driver; `tcp_reset_hosttest.c`, `packet_hosttest.c`, `exfat_hosttest.c` and `bcache_cost_hosttest.c`, each with a row that passed with a fault it should have seen; `tests/system/host_tools_mutate.sh`, whose interrupted cleanup stopped only its wrapper; `tests/system/runner/deadline.c`, which passed no signal on to the group it runs; `tools/dev/poll_kernels.sh`, whose private tree could not link a kernel; rows 6 to 6c of the installer suite, which raced the SSH server's start and took an ssh error for proof that no server answered; `tests/system/rows_mutate.sh`, which took any failure of a probe for its row's, with a `devprobe` fault that also wrote kernel memory; and the harness, whose `sendfile=` step passed when its file could not be read. Reproduce each as the record says. It breaks the rule that a row fails when its fault is present, and only then, and that a proof's cleanup ends before it puts a source back. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, each as the record gives it. Among the fixes, init prints `init: sshd: started as pid N` each time it forks the SSH server, which row 6c reads, and `deadline` passes on the stop signals it catches and waits for its group. On the Mac, `netutil_mutate.sh`, `tcp_reset_mutate.sh`, `ip_mutate.sh`, `bcache_mutate.sh`, `exfat_scan_mutate.sh` and `harness_input_mutate.sh` each passed with their new faults, `poll_kernels.sh` built both orders and matched its restored kernel, and `deadline` and `host_tools_mutate.sh`'s cleanup were run by hand under bash 3.2 and dash against a signal and exit, a member that cleans up on `TERM`, one that ignores it and one that is stopped. On the testing host's build from clean of 02:26 on 2026-09-26, `fat_barriers_mutate.sh` proved every barrier site but the flush in `fat_rmdir` and passed each restored driver; `recovery_disk_key_mutate.sh` failed row 6b with its init and installer faults and row 6c with its two server faults, and passed every restoration; `rows_mutate.sh` failed each probe's own row; `host_tools_mutate.sh` passed its seventeen cases; and `exfat_scan_mutate.sh`, `netutil_mutate.sh`, `tcp_reset_mutate.sh`, `ip_mutate.sh`, `bcache_mutate.sh` and `harness_input_mutate.sh` failed every fault and passed every restored source. The full set's `style` then found `deadline`'s `main` and the exFAT test's `write_rows` over their size limits; `main` was split into its setup and its wait loop, the two rows were moved into one call, and after that rebuild `exfat_scan_mutate.sh` passed again and the set passed all 90 suites.

### Milestone 54's close, 2026-09-27

The boots of the default suite on the batch's tree, the close's first full set and the build from clean found entries 227 to 235, with 231 found while fixing 230; the reviews of the eight areas' fixes found entries 236 to 252; and the full sets after them, the reviews of their fixes and the GNU system's rows found entries 253 to 262. Entry 263 is the part of 239 left once the calls that write were fixed. The close's leftovers, the work of 2026-09-28, fixed the entries the close had carried, all but three features of entry 261 and the cause of entry 262, and found entries 264 to 277, each fixed in the same work, and the machine phase of 2026-09-30 found and fixed entry 278. A file and line given for a contract is the tree's of 2026-09-28.

227. An AF_UNIX stream read at the end of input spun in the kernel, and stopped the machine (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the first boot of the default suite; severity P1: any process that read a stream socket after its peer closed took the processor for good, since a call is not preempted). `recv_stream` in `rach2/sys/ipc/unix.c` took `wait_data`'s end of input, 1, as success once the receive lock's rework, d840f56b, turned its `break` into `rc = 0`, and looped to wait again, which returned 1 at once. Reproduce by closing one end of a socketpair and reading the other. It breaks read(2)'s contract that a read at the end of input returns 0 (`man/man2/read.2:69`, for a stream socket). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, ed13a6f6: the loop ends at the end of input, and at a signal after data, and returns what it has; unixmusl's first row, which had hung the default suite and the sixteen probes after it, passes.

228. The kernel's source no longer built on Rach2 (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the install and self-build suites of the first full set; severity P2: the machine could not rebuild its own kernel, which its self-build and its manual promise). `sys/isr.c` read the SIMD status with `stmxcsr`, which the staged TinyCC's assembler does not know, so `make -f guest.mk` stopped at that file. Reproduce by running `tests/run selfbuild`. It breaks the rule that kernel code stays inside the dialect both compilers accept (`AGENTS.md:14`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, f0859c63: the instruction is written as its bytes, as `fpu.c` writes `ldmxcsr`. A TinyCC built for the host from the vendored source compiled the kernel's other 140 sources and the fixed `isr.c` without an error, and the self-build suites passed.

229. OpenSSH's server ended every connection, because `getsockopt` reported four bytes of IP options on each (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the gnu suite of the first full set; severity P2: no login to the GNU system over SSH was possible). `do_getsockopt` in `rach2/sys/syscall_net.c` answered every option it does not know with an integer 0, and OpenSSH's server reads `IP_OPTIONS` on each connection and ends one whose socket reports any, as it did once the fix of the review's B2-1 let its child run. Reproduce by reading `IP_OPTIONS` on an accepted TCP socket. It breaks setsockopt(2)'s answer for a socket with no options, which Linux gives as a length of 0 (`man/man2/setsockopt.2:106`). It depends on B2-1. Fixed here, 2673f6ce and ac793f8c: `IP_OPTIONS` answers a result of width 0 through the checks every option has; sockflagmusl requires it of an accepted connection, and the fault ip-options-four-bytes fails that row.

230. The stat calls took a path ending in `/` or `.` as naming its last component itself (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by direntmusl in the first boot of the default suite, and the rest by the review of the close's fixes; severity P3: `lstat("/proc/self/.")` reported the link, and `stat("file/")` the file). Each call copies the path and makes it canonical before the walk, which drops the trailing part that makes the last name a directory, followed even by a call that does not follow a final link, and a directory required. Reproduce by `lstat` of `/proc/self/.`, and by `stat` of a regular file with a slash after it. It breaks stat(2)'s rule for a path that names a directory by its end (`man/man2/stat.2:37`), which is Linux's. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 33733e3b and a8ff38f9: `copy_path_at` says whether the path as written names a directory by its end, and the stat calls, the old calls 106 and 107 among them, follow such a link and answer `ENOTDIR` when it reaches anything else; direntmusl's rows require both, and the faults lstat-dot-no-follow, stat-dir-end-not-checked and old-lstat-dot-no-follow fail them. The other calls that take a path are entry 231.

231. The calls that take a path, other than the stat calls, take one ending in `/` or `.` as naming its last component (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while fixing entry 230; severity P3: `open("file/")`, `unlink("file/")` and `chmod("file/")` act on the file where Linux answers `ENOTDIR`, and `rmdir("dir/.")` removes the directory where Linux answers `EINVAL`). Every such call copies its path with `copy_path_at` in `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c`, which makes it canonical before the walk, and only the stat calls ask it whether the path named a directory by its end (`path_from_user_dir`). Reproduce by opening a regular file with a slash after its name, or by removing an empty directory with `rmdir("dir/.")`. It breaks Linux's path resolution, which requires the last component of such a path to be a directory; the tree states the rule for the stat calls alone (`man/man2/stat.2:37`). It depends on entry 230, which gave the stat calls the answer. Fixed here, c16ded87, 170046fa, 0d60f417, 42e6afde, 0245f169, 1cb7f4df, 65b91b14, b3bb29e4, e14a8e40, 8262971b, b1126aa7, 6419fe73, 38abaf96, 97e1728f, b30a6433, 2a2fabaa, f81a68bc and 6504c3bd: each call that takes a path records how the path ends and walks a last `.`, so a lookup of `dir/.` needs search permission on `dir`, and answers as Linux does: `ENOTDIR` where the end reaches no directory, `EINVAL` and `ENOTEMPTY` from `rmdir` of a last `.` and `..`, `EBUSY` from `rename` and `rmdir` of the root, `EISDIR` for `O_CREAT` without `O_DIRECTORY` on a directory, `EINVAL` for `O_CREAT|O_DIRECTORY`, and `EEXIST` for a name that exists, a mount point among them, before the `EROFS`, `EPERM` or `EXDEV` of a call that makes a node and before the `EBUSY` of a RENAME_NOREPLACE rename. `execve`, a script's interpreter, `PT_INTERP`, `bind`, `connect` and `umount2` check the ending in the walk that opens what they name, so a link replaced between two walks cannot change the answer. Direntmusl's rows cover each call (0b451dc1, a9e2c742, 9b44a691, b7e017f2), with permmusl's search row and mvtest's rename of the root, and each row has a fault in `tests/system/probe_faults.txt`.

232. A stopped thread read as running in its stat file (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by ptracemusl's detach row in the second boot of the default suite; severity P3: `ps` and a debugger saw a stopped process as running). `proc_state_char` in `rach2/sys/fs/procfs.c` gave `R` for `PROC_STOPPED`, where the status file already said `T (stopped)` or `t (tracing stop)`. Reproduce by reading `/proc/PID/stat` of a process a SIGSTOP stopped. It breaks proc(5)'s agreement with Linux, whose state letters the page lists (`man/man5/proc.5:414`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, b0959283 and fec5f6fc; the fault stat-stopped-reads-running fails ptracemusl's row.

233. A transient init whose last thread `exit_group`'s SIGKILL ended was reported killed by that signal (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by threadmusl in the second boot of the default suite; severity P3: the probe runner scored a process that exited with 0 as failed). `terminate_current_process` in `rach2/kernel.c` printed the dying thread's own signal in the line the runner scores, where the process's status, which `proc_exit_cleanup` gives the leader, was the group's exit. Reproduce by ending a threaded program with `exit(0)` from its first thread under the debug shell. It breaks the runner's reading of that line as the probe's status (`tests/system/runner/runtests.c:6`). It depends on the review's K1-8 fix. Fixed here, 8f750831 and 01f1951d; the fault init-exit-by-thread-signal fails threadmusl's end.

234. A machine too small to hold the frame table panicked where the boot promises a refusal by name (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the memory suite of the first full set; severity P3: an 8 MiB machine stopped with an allocator's message and not the memory report's). `init_paging` in `rach2/sys/mm/paging.c` stopped with `the frame table lies over memory the map does not offer`, which milestone 54's larger recovery kernel reaches before the report's own check. Reproduce by booting the recovery pair on 8 MiB. It breaks the memory Readme's refusal by name (`rach2/sys/mm/Readme.md:7`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 64a13b7c: the case is refused by name in the memory report; the memory suite's 8 MiB row checks it. The same growth leaves 16 MiB with 7108 kB, under the 7 MiB `growroot` needs, so the grow suite runs on 32 MiB now (1a024862), and the smallest boot known to work is 12 MiB (e07b01c9).

235. OpenSSH's pre-authentication child runs with no sandbox (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the build from clean; severity P3: a flaw in the code that talks to a client before it authenticates runs as the `sshd` user in `/var/empty` with nothing further to hold it). The kernel has no seccomp, which the server's default sandbox needs, and configure takes the rlimit sandbox only after running a test program, which a cross build cannot, so `vendor/gnu/ports/openssh.sh` builds with `--with-sandbox=no` (8a8d1a2e). Reproduce by reading that script. It breaks nothing the manual promises; OpenSSH on Linux has a sandbox. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, d2583074, 935c0207 and 5031f33b: `vendor/gnu/ports/openssh.sh` answers configure's `select_works_with_rlimit` for the cross build and builds with `--with-sandbox=rlimit`, and limitprobe requires a child to `select` and `poll` under the limits the sandbox sets, a descriptor limit of 0 among them; the fault poll-refuses-past-descriptor-limit fails its row. The sandbox's limit of 0 on file growth is taken and not enforced, as prlimit64(2) says of every resource but its five.

236. `/proc/PID/exe` reaches the file at the program's path, which is not the program once the program is renamed or removed (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 5, its F3-6; severity P2: a program that runs `/proc/self/exe` again, or a root `chown` of it, can reach another file put at the old path). `exec_build` in `rach2/sys/syscall_proc.c` keeps the program's path in the new address space with `mm_set_exe`, and no reference to the file; `procfs_readlink_exe` in `rach2/sys/fs/procfs.c` gives that path, which `synth_readlink` in `rach2/sys/fs/vfs.c` hands the resolver as the link's target and `exec_follow_exe` puts in place of an `execve` of `/proc/self/exe`. Reproduce by running a program at `/tmp/a` that renames itself to `/tmp/b`, copies another program to `/tmp/a` and executes `/proc/self/exe`: the copy runs. It breaks Linux's link, which is to the program's file; proc(5) states the difference (`man/man5/proc.5:464`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, d854fe94, a352ffc5, 8b8bb7eb and 6b12744c: the address space keeps the program's file (`mm_exe_holds`), counted among a KRFFS file's holders and moved by `vfs_repoint`, and released under the root's lock; execve's load reads that file in place of its path, the resolver goes on from it, and `readlink` gives the file's present path, or the path the exec found it at with ` (deleted)` once its name is gone. Prctlmusl's rows run a renamed program's link, an open of the link once the name is gone, and an exec of `/proc/self/exe` with another file put at the old path; `procfs_hosttest.c` reads the link of a model process, and `synth_open_mutate.sh` and the faults exe-link-exec-path, exe-walk-text and exe-exec-old-path prove them.

237. An unlink or a rename on the KRFFS root counted the file's holders outside the root's namespace lock, so it could free the file an open was installing (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while fixing area 5's F2-2, and by the review of area 6; severity P1: on a KRFFS root read from a disk, the new descriptor kept a freed node and read and wrote whatever file the allocator put there). `do_unlink` and `do_rename` in `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c` looked the file up and counted its holders with `fd_node_holders` before they took `vfs_ns_lock`; an open holds that lock from its walk until it publishes its descriptor, and a descriptor still reserved is no holder, so when the open slept inside its walk, reading a page of the root from the disk, the call could count no holder and free the node once the open had published a descriptor on it. Reproduce with the root on a disk, one process that opens a file with `O_TRUNC` in a loop and another that unlinks it and makes it again. It breaks the root's holder rule, which `link(2)` states (`man/man2/link.2:73`). It depends on the disk-backed boot root. Fixed here, 875048f0 and 43253b76: `unlink_root_name` runs under the lock its walk took, and `rename_root_names` under the lock taken after both walks, each with its lookups, the count and the change in one hold; a rename walks both names with no lock, since a walk takes the lock of each mount it looks in and one held across the second walk could wait for another mount's lock, as a read through a loop device takes the root's. `open_hosttest.c`'s rows run an open that holds the lock inside each call, require each lookup and change under one hold of it and no walk begun holding one, and `open_mutate.sh`'s ten faults fail them (73723737, 4a4ef099).

238. Record locks on KRFFS are keyed by an inode number that is not the file's identity, so two files can share their locks and one file can have two sets (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while fixing area 5's F2-2, and by the review of area 6; severity P2: two processes can hold write locks over the same bytes of one file, and a lock on one file can refuse a lock on another). `open_install` in `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c` records an inode number in the description at the open, and `fd_lock_key` keys the record, description and flock locks by the mount and that number. On the KRFFS root the number is `krffs_node_ino`, the directory's id mixed with the hash of the file's name, which a rename rewrites and two names with one hash share, `bC` and `cb` among them; on a mounted KRFFS volume it is the node's offset plus one, which a write that moves the file changes. Reproduce by taking `F_SETLK` write locks on byte 0 of `/x/bC` and `/x/cb` from two processes, which refuses the second, or by locking a file, renaming it and locking the same bytes through a new open from another process, which succeeds. It breaks `fcntl(2)` and `flock(2)`, whose locks are on a file whatever its name, and the rule that `st_dev` and `st_ino` identify one file; `fcntl(2)` states the difference for a file renamed or moved (`man/man2/fcntl.2:222`), and not for two names with one hash. It depends on the format's rule that a regular file's id is its name's hash (entry 125). Fixed here, 21ab7a2b: a KRFFS file's record, description and flock locks are keyed by the place of its node (`fd_lock_key`), and `lock_repoint` moves them with the file when a write relocates it, as `vfs_repoint` moves descriptions, so two names with one hash have locks of their own and a rename or a move keeps them. Lockmusl's rows take locks through two names with one hash, across a rename and across a move, and the faults lock-key-root-by-ino-hash, lock-key-root-by-ino-rename and lock-repoint-missing fail them. The same work found entries 266, 267 and 268.

239. `chmod` and `chown` by path could change a node of a mounted KRFFS volume that a write had moved (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while fixing area 5's F2-5, and by the review of area 6; severity P1: root's `chmod` or `chown` could change another file's mode or owner, or write into another file's bytes). `do_chmod` and `do_chown` in `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c` resolved the path without `VFS_WALK_LOCK` and took `vfs_ns_lock` only for the attribute calls; a mounted KRFFS node is an offset (`handle_of` in `krffsmount.c`), on which the volume keeps no hold, so a growing write that held the lock and moved the file while one of them waited for it left the call the old offset, and `krffs_set_mode_at`, `krffs_set_owner_at` and `krffs_stamp_at` wrote over whatever was there then. Reproduce in `tests/host/krffsmount_hosttest.c`'s model, whose lock callbacks let a queued writer move a file after the call's walk and before its lock. It breaks `chmod(2)` and `chown(2)`, which change the file the path names (`man/man2/chmod.2:20`, `man/man2/chown.2:24`). It depends on the volume's numeric handles; `utimensat` walks with `VFS_WALK_LOCK` since the review's F2-5 and has no such window, and ext2, the FAT types and tmpfs hold their nodes. Fixed here, fb6f952c: each call walks with `VFS_WALK_LOCK` and keeps the lock through the check and the store; `open_hosttest.c`'s attribute rows run a chmod and a chown of a file that a write holding the lock moves, and the faults chmod-walk-unlocked and chown-walk-unlocked of `open_mutate.sh` fail them. The stat calls, which read, are entry 263.

240. A `/proc/PID/mem` descriptor keeps the whole address space of a process that has ended or executed another program, until it is next used or closed (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while fixing area 5's F3-1; severity P2: a user can hold the memory of as many ended children of their own as the descriptor limit allows and run the machine out of frames). `procfs_open_mem` in `rach2/sys/fs/procfs.c` takes a reference with `mm_get`, and `struct mm` has one count, whose last `mm_put` frees the pages, the regions and the page directory, so the descriptor's reference keeps everything after the process's exit or exec drops its own; `procfs_mem_mm` lets it go only at the next read or write. The same count makes `proc_thread_gone` in `rach2/sys/proc/futex.c` take the address space for shared while a descriptor holds it, which is harmless. Reproduce by forking a child that touches 16 MiB, opening its `/proc/PID/mem`, letting it exit and reaping it: `/proc/meminfo` stays 16 MiB lower until the descriptor is closed. It breaks Linux's reclaim, which frees an ended process's pages at its exit, and `proc(5)` does not say that a descriptor keeps them (`man/man5/proc.5:570`). It depends on the fix of F3-1 (bb594986), which gave the descriptor its reference. Fixed here, e1aacf47, 24176a2d, 6b1806b7 and 2a6199ba: `struct mm` counts users apart from holds (`mm_get`, `mm_get_live`, `mm_put`, `mm_hold`, `mm_unhold`); a `/proc/PID/mem` descriptor takes a hold, a read or write takes a user's count for its length only while a user remains, and a thread drops its address space at its end, before its zombie is published, so an ended process's pages and program's file go before the reap. Ptracemusl's exit row and prctlmusl's row of an ended child's program, `procfs_hosttest.c`'s rows of the open and the read, `synth_open_mutate.sh`, and the faults mem-descriptor-keeps-pages, exit-keeps-address-space-mem and exit-keeps-address-space-exe prove them.

241. A seek on a directory or on a `/proc` file answers `EBADF`, so `rewinddir` and `seekdir` move nothing and `lseek` cannot set a `/proc/PID/mem` offset (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found while writing area 5's rows for F3-1 and F3-5; severity P3: a program that rewinds a directory reads nothing the second time). `sys_lseek` and `sys__llseek` in `rach2/sys/syscall_fs.c` answer `EBADF` for `FD_TYPE_DIR`, `FD_TYPE_SYNTH_DIR` and `FD_TYPE_PROC`, and musl's `rewinddir` does not look at the answer. Reproduce by reading a directory to its end with `readdir`, calling `rewinddir` and reading again, which returns no entry. It breaks POSIX's `rewinddir` and `seekdir` and Linux's seek of `/proc/PID/mem`; `lseek(2)` lists the `EBADF` (`man/man2/lseek.2:68`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 378cc0b3 and 2b0c66a5: both seeks set a directory's cursor to the start of its listing or to a place `getdents64` gave, and a `/proc` file's offset to any byte, or address of `/proc/PID/mem`, under the description's lock, which a read or write of a `/proc` file now holds as well. Direntmusl's rewind and seekdir rows and preadmusl's rows of a `/proc` seek (30bd5825, 330a3a78) cover them, and the faults proc-seek-ebadf, old-lseek-listing-ebadf and proc-seek-cur-not-kept fail them.

242. Six of area 5's fixes have no row that proves them (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the record of area 5's fixes; severity P3: a fix that a later change could take back with every suite passing). F1-2 needs a process that shares the table to write through the core file's descriptor during the dump; F1-6 a core limit equal to the bytes a dump that ends in a hole writes before the zero it puts at the file's end; F2-4 a table with one free slot, which another thread sharing it takes while an open that would create or truncate sleeps in the file system; F2-5 a growing write on a mounted KRFFS volume that holds the lock while `utimensat` waits; F2-6 a framebuffer opened, used and closed by a thread that is not the first; and F3-2 a process that ends and is reaped while a read of its `cmdline` waits for a mapped page. Reproduce by reading the rows: taking a fix back (986a3ffe, 16cf9bb3, 123e4426, 923d2145, 4b38d60c, 402dcc11) fails none. It breaks the rule that a fix is trusted once a test has failed without it (`AGENTS.md:52`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here: F1-2 by 89458521, a `corefile_hosttest.c` row in which a process sharing the table writes through the core file's descriptor during the dump; F1-6 by 20c67838 and f63e3080, coremusl's limit set to the bytes a dump that ends in a hole writes before its last zero, with the fault core-zero-past-limit; F2-4 and F2-5 by b680f8e1, `open_hosttest.c` rows that run another thread's open in the table's last free slot while an open sleeps, and a growing write while `utimensat` waits; F2-6 by e46ef303 and c561dc1c, threadmusl's rows that open, use and close the framebuffer from a second thread, with the faults fb-open-thread-id, fb-read-thread-id and fb-close-thread-id; and the argument read by 0fb55147, a `procfs_hosttest.c` row that reaps the process while the read sleeps for a page. `corefile_mutate.sh`, `open_mutate.sh` and `synth_open_mutate.sh` prove the host rows.

243. A `mkdir` that KRFFS refuses because another directory has the new directory's identifier answers `ENOSPC` on the boot root and `EIO` on a mounted volume (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 6; severity P3: an error that names a full or a failing volume for a name the format cannot hold). `krffs_mkdir_at` in `rach2/sys/fs/krffs.c` answers `KRFFS_EBAD` for a directory whose identifier, its parent's mixed with its name's hash, another directory has; `kfs_mkdir` in `vfs.c` answers `ENOSPC` for every failure of the core and `kv_mkdir` in `krffsmount.c` `EIO` for every result but `KRFFS_ENOSPACE`. Reproduce by making the directories `/x/bC` and `/x/cb`, whose names have one hash. It breaks `mkdir(2)`, where `ENOSPC` is a full file system and `EIO` a failed device (`man/man2/mkdir.2:161`, `:178`). It depends on entry 125, which added the refusal. Fixed here, 8c97ee95: the core answers `KRFFS_ETAKEN` for a taken identifier, and both adapters pass it on as `EINVAL`, which `mkdir(2)` and the KRFFS Readme state. Dirprobe's row makes `/ids/bC` and `/ids/cb` and requires `EINVAL`; the fault krffs-root-mkdir-taken-is-enospc fails it, and `krffs_id_mutate.sh` proves the host rows of both adapters.

244. A KRFFS volume writes a moved node's mode, owner and times, and a new node's times, under the whole-block rule, so a node placed across a block can fail to be moved or made (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 6; severity P2: a write that has to move its file answers `ENOSPC` on a volume with room, at each try, and a creation there can answer `EIO`). `krffs_allocate_err` in `rach2/sys/fs/krffs.c`, filling the copy a move makes, writes its mode, owner and three times with the calls that refuse a field whose changed bytes fall in two blocks (`krffs_write_is_whole`), and `krffs_create_node_at` and `krffs_mkdir_at` write a new node's access time the same way; the copy is a free node until its type is written last, and a new node has no name until its name is written last, so no reader could see those fields torn. The fields at bytes 267 to 324 of a node cross a 4096-byte block at 52 of every 4096 places. Reproduce in `tests/host/krffsmount_hosttest.c`'s model with a clock whose nanoseconds are not zero: a node placed at offset 3811 answers `EIO` to its creation, and a file that has to move there `ENOSPC` to its write. It breaks `write(2)` and `open(2)`, where `ENOSPC` means a full file system (`man/man2/write.2:289`, `man/man2/open.2:563`). It depends on entry 15, which took a new node's ids out of the rule. Fixed here, daadc4b2: `krffs_put_new` writes each field of a node being made, and of the copy a move fills, without the whole-block rule, since no reader can see the node before its publishing write. `krffs_hosttest.c` makes a node across a block and moves a file there, and `krffs_id_mutate.sh` puts the rule back in each path.

245. `recvfrom` given an address and a null length pointer returns the data, where Linux fails the call with `EFAULT` (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 7; severity P3: a caller that passes no length pointer is not told of its error). `do_recvfrom` in `rach2/sys/syscall_net.c` and `unix_recvfrom` in `rach2/sys/ipc/unix.c` settle the sender's address only when both pointers are given. Reproduce by calling `recvfrom(fd, buf, sizeof(buf), 0, (struct sockaddr *)&from, NULL)` on a datagram socket with a message queued. It breaks Linux's contract, which `accept` keeps since cd9ef4b1; `recv(2)` says that no address is returned when either pointer is null (`man/man2/recv.2:84`), and not that Linux fails the call. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, c8e890c7: both functions fail the call with `EFAULT` before the data is taken, so the message stays queued, and `recv(2)` says so. Unixmusl's and loopprobe's rows require the error and then the message, and the faults unix-recvfrom-no-length-efault, unix-recvfrom-no-length-queued, inet-recvfrom-no-length-efault and inet-recvfrom-no-length-queued fail them.

246. An edge-triggered epoll registration is reported again after any wake of its descriptor's queue, where Linux reports it only for a change among the events it watches (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 7; severity P3: an edge-triggered loop is told of an edge that did not come, and finds `EAGAIN`). The review's N3-1 fix, 2a9461a2, has `item_ready` and `item_woken` in `rach2/sys/io/epoll.c` report an edge-triggered registration again when its queue has counted a wake since the last report, and `wq_wake_all` counts every wake, while one queue carries changes of more than one kind: an AF_UNIX socket's data, room and shutdown. Reproduce by registering one end of an AF_UNIX stream pair for `EPOLLOUT | EPOLLET`, taking the first report, sending a byte to that end, and waiting again: the wait reports `EPOLLOUT` again. It breaks Linux's edge-triggered contract with a report too many; none is lost. `epoll(2)` gives the difference (`man/man2/epoll.2:70`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 48a83476, f4b14adc, ff71430c, 9cfd9aa6, cd8f458a and ed4d97e3: a wait queue counts its wakes for each kind of change, data, room or hangup (`wq_wake`, `WQ_DATA`, `WQ_ROOM`, `WQ_HANGUP`), each waker names its kind, and an edge-triggered registration compares the count of the kinds it watches. `epoll_hosttest.c`, `wait_queue_hosttest.c`, `socket_wake_hosttest.c` and `raw_hosttest.c` check the kinds, and epollmusl's rows, among them a pipe written twice, a shutdown, a pty master's room and TCP and UDP arrivals, fail under the faults of their group. The review of the fix found entries 272 and 273.

247. An epoll set can be nested more than four sets deep from the outside in, and a wait on the outermost set then never reports what the deepest set watches (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 7; severity P3: a wait sleeps through events of a nesting Linux would have refused). The review's N3-8 fix, 1e74e57e, has `set_ready` in `rach2/sys/io/epoll.c` look four sets deep at most, and `EPOLL_CTL_ADD` refuse with `ELOOP` a set that would make a cycle or holds sets nested too deep; `set_loops` counts only the sets below the one added, not those above the set it is added to. Reproduce by making six sets, adding each to the one before, the outermost first, a pipe to the sixth, and waiting on the first after a write: every add succeeds and the wait reports nothing. It breaks `epoll(2)`, which says such an add fails with `ELOOP` (`man/man2/epoll.2:123`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, eda7f772 and 6683013f: the add counts the sets above the set added to, through every table that holds a registration of it, and the longest path of a set held by two, and refuses a nesting past four with `ELOOP`; a registration lasts while its open file is open in any process, so a child that closed its copy of a number cannot hide a set from the count. `epoll_hosttest.c` nests sets from above and below and through a fork, and epollmusl's nesting rows fail under the faults epoll-nest-above-uncounted, epoll-nest-five-refused, epoll-nest-scan-four-sets, epoll-nest-below-cut-early, epoll-nest-below-uncounted, epoll-nest-shorter-path, epoll-nest-above-by-table, epoll-nest-below-by-table and epoll-gone-by-table.

248. An overrun of a process's kernel stack is not caught, so a row whose fault overruns it ends the boot without printing its line (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 7; severity P3: a kernel defect that recurses past 16 KiB writes over the heap pages below the stack with no message, or resets the processor). `kstack_alloc` in `rach2/sys/syscall_proc.c` takes each 16 KiB kernel stack from `kalloc_pages` with no unmapped page below it, and vector 8, the double fault, has an interrupt gate in `rach2/sys/tables/idt.c`, which runs on the stack in use. Unixmusl's closed chain of 1000 sockets and tmpfsmusl's deep unmount are proved only by a boot that ends. Reproduce by reading `kstack_alloc` and the gate of vector 8. It breaks `init_isr`'s contract that a fault in kernel mode yields a diagnostic and a panic (`rach2/sys/isr.c:234`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, e8f29e9a, fb8d07cd and 20873448: each kernel stack has an unmapped guard page below it (`kalloc_guarded`), and the double fault has a task gate with a stack of its own, whose handler names the thread and stops the machine. The `rach2.stackoverrun` switch runs a stack out at boot, from a forked thread and from the boot stack, and the memory suite's rows require the thread named and the machine stopped; `tests/system/stackoverrun_mutate.sh` compiles three faults into private kernels, each of which fails them. The work found entry 265.

249. Eight fixes in the AF_UNIX and pipe calls have no row that proves them (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 7; severity P3: a change that undid one would pass every suite). They are the descriptors of `accept`, `socketpair`, an `SCM_RIGHTS` receive, TCP's `accept` and `pipe2` reserved until their numbers reach the caller (230f4037, a0533e0a; N1-1, N2-4, N5-1); `connect` judged after its last sleep (24ab7f58, N1-2); the bind lock (a01ccb7a, N1-3); the receive lock (d840f56b, N2-1); descriptors in flight counted by the node their description names (c982aa41, a8f7822c, N2-3); and a send judged again after its copy (aa05fe76, N2-6). All but N2-3 close a window in which another thread's call lands while this one sleeps in a page fault or in the file system; a probe cannot place a call inside such a window, and no host test compiles `unix.c`. N2-3 has two parts: c982aa41's count by the node shows in steps a probe can take one after another, and a8f7822c's, which keeps a descriptor counted after a receive takes it off the queue while its installation or cleanup sleeps, needs a concurrent call like the others. Reproduce by reading the probes and the host tests. It breaks the rule that a fix is trusted once a test has failed without it (`AGENTS.md:52`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 6828dbfe, d81b57a9, fe230b0b, 662b03d5, 12b20d53 and 26548442: `unix_hosttest.c` compiles `unix.c` and `pipe.c` with a model whose user copy, socket-file lookup and last release sleep and run another thread's call there (`compat/park_host.c`), and a sibling that must wait is resumed in its wait only after a wake of its queue; its rows cover each fix but TCP's, which `netif_hosttest.c`'s accept row covers, and `unix_mutate.sh` and `netif_mutate.sh` compile a fault of each. Unixmusl's row sends a KRFFS file's descriptor, moves the file with a write, closes and unlinks it, and receives and reads it, with the faults unix-inflight-count-by-node and unix-inflight-install-by-node.

250. Two rows that this close's kernel fixes added waited a fixed time where they should see their waiter asleep, and passed without the wait they test when the waiter ran late (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 8; severity P3: on a loaded host each row passed with its fault in the kernel). `closed_wait` in `tests/musl/lockmusl.c` paused 200 ms before closing the descriptor a thread's F_SETLKW waited through, and `full_receiver_shut` in `tests/musl/unixmusl.c` 100 ms before shutting down the receiver a child's send waited on. It breaks the rule that a suite judges what the kernel counts, never the clock (`AGENTS.md:53`). It depends on nothing. Fixed here, d0bf8213 and 73c971ac: each row acts once its waiter is seen asleep in `/proc/.../stat`, and fails with a message of its own when it never is.

251. `waitprobe`'s interrupted-wait row signalled its child after a fixed 50 ms, and a child that ran late failed the row or stopped the default run (2026-09-27, milestone 54's close, found by the review of area 8; severity P3: a failure with no fault in the kernel, or a run that stops at `waitprobe` with every later probe unrun).
