The earlier ten-minute complete install is no longer described as a ten-minute grow; neither the alignment patch's physical speedup nor the old install's time distribution is inferred.

* **The residuals the close must dispose of.** Entry 36's race is real and fixed: `rach2/sys/usb/hub.c` records a port's status when it reads it, and the acknowledgement of those change bits goes out a tick or more later, after the whole scan, so a port that read as disconnected with a change already latched and that connects during the scan has the new device's latch cleared unread, and the core's pass at `core.c:1589` then sees a record that says disconnected and skips it forever. The driver now reads a port's status once more after acknowledging its change bits, and `rach2/sys/usb/Readme.md` says so. Proved by a row in `tests/host/hub_hosttest.c` where a device lands on a port the instant after its status is read: the row passes as the source stands, fails with the re-read never asked for, and passes again restored. `vendor/e2fsprogs/Readme.md` is corrected: two programs are staged, `e2fsck` and `resize2fs`, the configure enables the resizer, and the size limits the root `Makefile` enforces are stated. The load harness is in the tree as `tools/dev/machine_load.sh` rather than in a scratch directory, and it starts its sessions a second apart: Dropbear allows five unauthenticated connections from one address at a time, which is `MAX_UNAUTH_PER_IP` in the port's `default_options.h` and is not changed by `localoptions.h`, so twenty started together get five. The ext2 writeback contract in `rach2/sys/fs/ext2.Readme.md` was rewritten when the batch landed in `aecb6f1b`. The pause between load sessions has its row since 2026-09-24: the three-session case of `tests/system/machine_measure_test.sh` requires the driver to pause a second after each session starts, and a mutation that removes the pause fails it.

* **55, SSH inbound. Done.** The cause is the card's message discipline and it is named, measured and repaired above: the handler now masks the sources while it runs and unmasks at its end. SSH inbound 22.3 MB/s against 2.4, a raw socket 118.3 against 6.4, outbound unchanged. The kernel that measured it is the one this tree builds, installed through `bootctl` and committed on the machine. What is left of the gap between SSH and a raw socket is Dropbear's own path, 22.3 in against 35.7 out, which is the cost of the protocol and not a defect; the finding is in `notes.md`. Every inbound measurement taken in this milestone before 2026-09-13 was taken on a machine whose card had fallen into the tick's pace, and the later logs do not identify their running kernel. Entry 54 withdraws its claimed per-kernel comparison; unit D adds no new one to the sitting.

### Phase 7: the close, run again

The milestone closed once at `01d02656` and was reopened by the second sitting, so the close runs again, once, in this order: finish phase 6's fixes with the small proof each one owes; check what would stop a long step, the plan, the notes and any job still running; build from clean, with `e2fsprogs` rebuilt and the recovery artifacts shown to carry its new binaries; `tests/run all`, its width, its lone group, its calibration and the hashes of what it booted written down; fix everything that run finds; run the prose check; give the diff since `01d02656` to Codex in units grouped by contract, six files at most each, each unit given the interfaces its diff depends on, most serious first; fix every finding or reject it in `notes.md` with the source that shows it wrong; `tests/run all` again. Then the machine: the prepared recovery row for entry 53, run after the review of the code that performs its write, and any fix it produces reviewed and proved before acceptance. Deploying a kernel through `bootctl` carries none of the changed userland, so the probes, `resize2fs` and Dropbear reach the machine by the paths the Readme's "Installing and updating a machine" gives, and the record says which was used for each. Then the records: `notes.md` has the second sitting and its measurements, the milestone's Readme takes the accepted commit, the build options, the image hashes, the runs and the hardware arrangement again, and the index entry is rewritten to the finished state. The machine ends on a kernel built from the accepted commit, installed through `bootctl` and committed, with the acceptance passing.

Two changes made to work the machine are not part of the tree and are undone at the close: `network_access` in `~/.codex/config.toml`, which let a Codex unit reach the machine, and the demonstration key in root's `authorized_keys` on the notebook, which let it work without the password file.

### Phase 8: the first run

The third sitting reported five things about what a person meets the first time they use this system, and they are one piece of work rather than five entries: a line of coloured text at the end of every boot that vanished before it could be read, the kernel's checks costing about a second of every boot, messages still arriving on the screen after the login prompt, an installer that never said what it was or what it would do, and boot menus of eight and three entries written in words only this project uses.

What was decided, with the owner, and built.

**The screen has three phases.** The kernel writes to it during boot and stops when a real `/sbin/init` starts; after that only a panic reaches it. Everything still goes to the ring, the serial port and netconsole, so nothing is lost, and `/dev/screenlog` reads and sets the level. Three boots keep their messages: one whose line named a level, a `rach2.fsck` recovery boot, which has minutes of work to show, and a read-only root, which the owner needs to know about before working on the machine. `power_stop` takes the screen back, because a machine that has stopped must be able to say so and there is no log to read afterwards. The screen is no longer cleared at init, so the login prompt appears under the boot the person has just read.

**The boot log is what a person can act on.** About a hundred records moved to debug: the lines that only say a step of initialisation finished, the per-port USB detail, the message-signalling vectors, the card identity dumps and the counters that are all zero at bring-up. The `X: started` lines for slow work stayed, because a boot that hangs is named by the line that did not finish. 122 visible lines became about 25 in the emulator; the Lenovo's boot at `e1eec90c` put 48 of its 110 records on the screen (`notes.md`).

**The checks at boot are the ones only the hardware can answer.** Eleven went: memory, heap, strings, paging, system calls, context switching, isolation, round robin, KRFFS write, ELF load, page cache. Each is exercised by the first program that starts and covered by the host tests and the probe suites; the memory one read 256 bytes and the page-cache one ran against a RAM disk. Three stayed, run at every boot, and print their measurements rather than a verdict:

    timing: tick 100 Hz, 200 ms delay measured 200 ms, 2000 us wait measured 2001 us

A machine whose delays run short can still boot and log in, so a boot alone does not show that its delays are right. The clock check found them short on this laptop, where an interrupt handler latched the PIT in the middle of a sample and a 2000 us wait measured 1361 us (`dev-log/43-machine/notes.md`).

**The menus name what they do.** The stick has one entry, `Install Rach2`: installing is why a person boots it, and repairing is what that system offers once they are in. The installed disk has three, all of them saying Rach2 because all three are Rach2, each of the other two saying how it differs:

    Start Rach2
    Start Rach2 with the previous kernel
    Start the Rach2 recovery system

Earlier drafts failed on exactly that. "Repair this computer" promises the boot repairs something; "Start the repair tools" promises tools start; "Start the recovery system" reads as not-Rach2. Five entries went. The kernel debug shell came off both menus, since it authenticates nobody and was a root shell one keypress from an installed machine's boot menu; its code stays, because the test image boots into it and 22 of the 44 suites depend on that. The check-disks entry went, since it set a boot token to run `e2fsck` before anything else and `e2fsck` is a program a person runs from recovery. The three fixed-address entries went, since a fixed network address is a property of where a machine lives and not a choice made at every boot.

Removing that pair removed the machinery serving it: `running_static_entries`, `bootslot_use_static_entries`, the `-static` decoration of entry ids, and the `set fallback=3` in `grub.cfg`. That last one mattered. GRUB 2.12 parses `fallback` as a number and discards a name, so the order of the entries is part of the file's contract, and the draft that replaced the number with an id would have disabled the automatic fallback silently. Codex found that before it was written.

**The disk has three partitions.** The KRFFS root is gone: a second complete system with the demonstration accounts, which the fallback entry booted so a machine came up to something when the installed root would not mount. The recovery system does that and more, from RAM, with the tools to repair the machine. So the fallback slot now guards the one failure GRUB detects by itself, a kernel image that will not load, and boots the person's own root with it, which is also what makes the entry nameable.

The two FAT partitions stay separate. A merge was considered and rejected: GRUB's core lives in the gap before the first partition and can reach either file system, the boot partition is written at every kernel install and every trial while the recovery partition is written at an install and an update, and `grub-embed.cfg` already falls through to the second when the first cannot be read. Keeping the rescue path out of the volume that gets written is protection against the likeliest damage.

**The installer speaks to a person.** It greets, says it writes nothing until the last question, and says how to stop. No question ends the run: a wrong answer says what is wrong and asks again, since nothing is written until the confirmation. Disks are numbered. Text addressed to a person carries no `rach2-install: ` prefix, which is a diagnostic's, and no line runs past 80 columns: the key prompt was 90, the no-keys summary 85 with a legal account name, and the disk list row 132.

What this phase still owes, after a review of the plan by Codex and the owner's decisions on what it found. The review opened with "I would not build phase 8 as written", and six of its findings changed the work.

**The interrupted update has nowhere to land, and that is a regression this phase caused.** `rach2-install` saves the `fallback` entry before it overwrites the root and arms `candidate` afterwards. That was safe while the fallback booted the KRFFS root; both entries now boot the ext2 root, so a power cut during the write leaves neither usable, where before the machine still came up. The installer must save `recovery` before any root write, keep it saved through the write and the readback, and let only an explicit commit make the candidate permanent. The second FAT partition does not cover this: `grub-embed.cfg` reaches it when the boot partition cannot be read, and a readable menu loading a kernel whose root is broken never goes down that path.

**Two disk targets are leftovers and two are live suites.** `os-real-grub-disk` and `rach2-metal-grub-disk` are from before the installer existed, when a machine got Rach2 by imaging a disk over a USB adapter and swapping it in; they go, with their documentation. `os-real-grub-disk` came back in milestone 53 (entry 112) for `rach2/run_from_disk.sh`, the one path that boots a disk through the BIOS, the MBR and GRUB by hand, and stays, with a test of its rule. `run-grub` and `run-drill` are the `grub` and `drill` suites, `grub` in the core tier, and they built disks whose root was the KRFFS partition. They need a root supplied, as `run-drill-ext2` already does, and the builder must refuse to build a disk with no root rather than produce one that boots nothing.

**Recovery has no login, on the stick and on the disk, and starts its SSH server only when root has an authorized key.** The password this phase planned would not have been a boundary: the recovery image still stages the demonstration keys and starts SSH regardless of what happens at the console, and a person at the GRUB menu can still add `rach2.rescue` to a boot line. Closing only the menu entry would have made the recovery system look closed while those two ways in stayed open. The archived account files cannot authenticate either: they are refreshed at an install and an update and not when root runs `passwd`, so they can hold a password the owner has replaced; they carry nothing that says which installation they came from; and `write_archive` removes the old copy before renaming the new one, so a stop between the two leaves none. They stay what they were built to be, the copy that puts accounts back after an update. The archive has since been rebuilt: entry 162 put the keys in it, and entries 163 and 167 replaced the rename with two slots that are never renamed.

So both recovery systems boot to a shell, a recovery image has a key only when it is built with one, and the documentation says plainly that physical access to a machine is control of it. The installed system is unchanged: SSH with the account's key, and root by password.

Whether a recovery system answers the network is decided by one rule, and the rule is not a mode: **the SSH server starts only where there is an authorized key, so no key means no server.** A credential is a build input rather than a property of the code, which is what makes this work for the three cases at once. A stick built for development has the owner's key and updates a machine remotely the way it does now. A stick built for anybody else has none, starts nothing, and is reachable only after a person puts a key on it. The recovery partition of an installed disk has none either, so the installer offers to place one; the recovery system of a disk given none starts an SSH server only when its image was built with a key. The offer was built on 2026-09-24: `rach2-install` asks for a public key for the recovery system and writes it to `boot/recovery_authorized_keys` on the recovery partition, and the recovery init, on a boot from that partition's entry only, adds it to root's keys (rows 6b and 6c of `tests/run installer`, and `tests/system/recovery_disk_key_mutate.sh`).

This matters because a root update cannot be done from the running system: the update overwrites the whole root partition and the kernel refuses to open a partition whose file system is mounted, with `EBUSY`, which is the fault `tests/run drill` proves. So a root update runs from recovery, and a recovery that answers nothing is a machine that can only be updated at its keyboard. A kernel update is not affected, since `bootctl` writes the boot partition.

The danger of that rule is that a development image and a release image are identical from outside, so the difference has to be visible. Two guards are built here: the machine says at boot whether a key was built into it, and a development build writes its image under a name that says so. A third, a build that refuses to produce a release image while a key is present, is the real guard, and was built on 2026-09-24: `make release` builds the install stick and the recovery ISO, and it and `RELEASE=1` are refused before anything is built in a tree with `krffs/recovery_authorized_keys`. The sealed and GRUB disks, which carry the recovery image, were named for the key the same day; the rows `key-names` and `release-guard` of `tests/system/build_rules_test.sh` hold both.

**The `sealed` suite is rewritten, not renumbered.** It damages the ext2 root and then requires a working KRFFS system, and later destroys and repairs that partition. Those rows are about the thing this phase removed. They become what the machine now does: damage the root, land in recovery, repair from there.

**Two of the three known problems block the close.** The metal kernel is built by a phony target while the disk images depend on the file it leaves behind, with no rule between them, so editing `kernel.c` does not rebuild the kernel that ships; it shipped a stale one today, caught by reading a boot log rather than by a check. And `/var/log/services` must have a bound within a boot, since a service that fails in a loop can fill an installed root or a recovery system's memory; `tests/run servicelog` holds the bound, and passed in the close's first set; `tests/system/servicelog_mutate.sh` is its mutation, which passed on the testing host on 2026-09-25. The AHCI second per empty port stays measured and accepted.

**Machines on the old layout are not migrated.** `--update` keeps the partition table, so it cannot move an installation from four partitions to three, and the new menu must never be applied to the old layout. The owner's decision is that the next stick installs fresh over whatever is there.

**Two faults of the build itself came out of the close, both of the same shape.** A component was reachable by two routes with no edge between them, so `make -j` took both: the name `rach2` ran a sub-make and the kernel's file rule ran another, and a clean parallel build compiled every kernel object twice into the same files and linked one that a second job was still writing (`undefined reference to krandom_seed`). `ish` had it too, and the three KRFFS tools share one rule, which make reads as a rule per target, so three missing files were three sub-makes of one directory. Each component now has one file rule that asks its sub-make, and the component name asks for that file. The second fault was the allowlist: the two init programs were declared against each other's manifest, so no image could be staged at all, and `utils/guest.mk` had no rule for `init-recovery`, so the guest build of the utilities did not build it. Both are the kind a clean build finds and an incremental one hides.

**The string sweep came back as a boot option.** It was one of the eleven checks removed from every boot, and the only exhaustive exercise of the word moves in `sys/lib/kstring.c`, which are i386 instructions the host test beside that file cannot run. `rach2.strings` runs it: 68 lengths at four source and four destination alignments, copy, fill and move both ways, with the bytes around each range required to be untouched. `tests/run memory` boots it once. The copy's byte tail removed reports the first length that is not a multiple of four; restored, it passes again.

Then the suites that name a partition number, `tests/run all`, a review of the whole diff, and the stick.

## Risks

* Notify the owner when a severe problem is added. One entry that loses data matters more than twenty that do not.
* The sitting is the first run of five milestones' kernel changes on real hardware, and the new recovery image shares them. A new stick that does not boot ends the sitting at step 3 with the disk untouched. A boot that stops before SSH has the netconsole capture and the screen; the boot switches skip individual probes, and polled AHCI is a boot option. A damaged install is restored from the new stick, or from milestone 43's stick if the new one no longer boots, with the written commands.
* The save writes the image's extent and the growth ranges to the stick, the path that once failed. The hash back is the check.
* An interruption during the image copy leaves the disk's own loader and recovery partition half written; the stick is the boot device then, and its recovery is what boots. Milestone 49's rows prove both boundaries.
* Three sittings: the first, the second of 2026-09-12, and the short recovery sitting phase 7 needs for entry 53. A hardware failure that needs the owner's hands calls a third, prepared the same way, and is reported as such. More sittings ran than this planned; `notes.md` and `dev-log/06-review/results-2026-09-20.md` record each.

## What is still open

Milestone 54's close found entries 227 to 263, its leftovers entries 264 to 277, and its machine phase entry 278; every one is fixed but two, each P3. Entry 261 keeps three features no suite's row needs, the write-back of shared writable file mappings, the inotify calls and a netlink route socket, for the owner to schedule; Chromium runs without them. Entry 262 keeps its cause, likely entry 277's, which the next failure's kept logs will show; the suite passed in the ten runs after it.

Before milestone 54 no work was open. Milestone 06's close of 2026-09-24 wrote the rows and mutations the audit of that day found owed and fixed the faults entries 170 to 203 record; its runs of 2026-09-25 found and fixed entries 204 to 214, the reviews of that day's changes found entries 215 to 222, the owner found entry 223 at the machine's console, and the Codex review of 2026-09-26 found entries 224 to 226, each fixed before the close ended. `dev-log/06-review/results-2026-09-20.md` records what was accepted.

What the list keeps as a known limit, each in its own entry:

* Entry 11: a disk whose logical sectors are 4096 bytes is refused by name, since the block layer counts in 512-byte sectors, as milestone 47's plan allows while the refusal is reported.
* Entry 16: closed as inferred. No row reproduces the warm restart's fault, so no mutation can be made of it.
* Entry 32: `fat_rmdir`'s flush fails no row when dropped, by construction, since `free_chain` repeats it with no write between; the barrier proof names it.
* Entry 110: the index over the KRFFS ring is not built; the page cache's count row says when the ring outgrows the cache.
* Entry 174: no mutation, since a count wraps to a pass only at a multiple of 256 failures.
* Entries 196 and 197: exec's relocation window and three of the harness's input rules have no row of their own; each fix is the form a proved fix already has.
* Entry 212: `ifconfig` cannot list the interfaces, since the kernel does not answer `SIOCGIFCONF`, and the manual says so. The interval timer counts real time alone, and a call its signal interrupts is not restarted (`setitimer(2)`, `sigaction(2)`).
* Entry 213: two stamps taken back to back can be equal when the emulator's tick arrives after its counter wraps; they never go back.
* Entry 51: `orphanprobe`, which must be the init process, runs in QEMU's default suite at every set and not on the installed machine, by the owner's decision of 2026-09-25.
* Entries 215 and 216: the sleep and the timer count whole 10 ms ticks, so a timer's value read in its last tick and passed straight back comes due a tick later, and a sleep begun again after a signal in its last tick can end up to a tick before the time first asked (`setitimer(2)`, `nanosleep(2)`).
* Entry 219: a stop signal that takes its default action ends a sleep, or another interruptible wait, as any other signal does, and the call returns when the process is continued, where Linux restarts most such calls (`sigaction(2)`, `nanosleep(2)`).
* Entry 226: `deadline` does not pass on a stop signal that comes after it has decided to return its command's status, as it does not pass on one sent after it has ended; its header says so.
* Entry 247: an epoll wait sees a registration only through the caller's own descriptor at its number, since a wait sleeps on the queues of what it looks at and only the caller's descriptors keep those; a process that closed the number is told nothing of the registration, which lasts while its file is open anywhere (`epoll(2)`).
* Entries 56, 65 and 83 keep the unknowns their texts state, and entries 101, 103, 164, 165, 169 and 222 were proved by a mutation made by hand and recorded in the entry rather than by a script in the tree.

What was run at the end. On 2026-09-25, on the testing host: the mutation tests stopped or not reached on 2026-09-24 and those whose scripts or code changed after, and each passed, some after a fault of its own was fixed, and a test that a neighbour editing the tree had disturbed, or that found the tree's lock taken, ran again alone; the rehearsal of `manual_probes.sh` against the sealed disk in the emulator; the GRUB disk of entry 112; the baseline's re-run in its own tree; and thirteen full sets. The sets at 06:58, 08:37 and 10:10 by that host's clock each passed 89 of 90, `style` failing on findings of the close's own changes, each fixed. After the fixes of entries 215 to 219, two builds from clean, at 14:34 and 15:08, each with the mutation tests of those entries and `rows_mutate.sh` passed and `tests/run all` passing all 90 suites; and after the rounds of the reviews the owner asked for, six more, at 18:52, 19:27, 20:03, 20:59, 22:08 and 23:32, each with `tests/run all` passing all 90 suites and the mutation tests of what the round changed passed; and after the Codex review of 2026-09-26 a seventh, at 02:26 that morning, with every mutation script the review changed or added passed, whose set failed `style` on four size findings and, once they were fixed, passed all 90 suites, as `results-2026-09-20.md` lists. At the Lenovo that night and the next morning: four updates, the first through `bootctl` for the kernel and the recovery system for the stick and the recovery partition, the second, for entry 223, and the third, for the sixth round's pages, through the recovery system for the stick alone, and the fourth, for the Codex review's fixes, through the recovery system for the stick and the recovery partition, each written from there and read back equal to its image, and each through `rach2-install --update` for the root, which then wrote its configuration archive onto the recovery partition; on each trial boot, before `bootctl commit`, `metal_accept.sh`, the probes of entry 51 and `arping` ending at its deadline, and on the first the measurements of entries 26 and 49; the owner's pull of the stick under a read, above; and with the owner there, earlier that day, the PC speaker, Doom's sound under a network load, and the keyboard and the mouse in the blue socket without the hub.

Nothing is left at the machine. The owner pulled the stick under a read on 2026-09-25 and put it back, the replug of the pull test of Phase 3's step 8 that the first sitting's record does not have, and waived the console run of `orphanprobe`, entry 51's last probe, as that entry says.

The fixes of the closing review's fifteen findings, and those of entries 199 to 223 made after it, were first read by reviewers of the session: three the day's changes, two their fixes and a sixth the records, and then six rounds, twenty-seven reviewers in all, each the fixes of the round before; `notes.md` says what each round found. On 2026-09-26 the owner had them reviewed by Codex, at most three Astra units at the same time, with the close's prose, which the session had written: a first round of five units over everything that changed after the closing review, and then a round over the fixes of each round before, until the fifth found nothing to change. The rounds found 48, 12, 7, 4 and no faults, among them entries 224 to 226; [codex-2026-09-26.md](../06-review/codex-2026-09-26.md) gives each finding and what became of it.

300. tmux started at a console login left the console as it was, with the keyboard's keys going nowhere, while the machine ran on (2026-10-05, milestone 57's machine, found by the owner at the Lenovo; severity P1: the owner took the machine for hung and restarted it). The kernel gave the first process its standard descriptors on the console as three descriptions, fd 0 read-only and fds 1 and 2 write-only, and a login inherits them; tmux's client passes its standard input to the server, which draws through it, and the console answered POLLOUT only on a descriptor open for writing, so the server polled forever with nothing drawn. Every check of tmux ran it detached, over SSH, or on the console opened read-write. Reproduce under QEMU with a root login at the console and `tmux`; `/proc/2/fdinfo/0` shows flags 04000. It breaks what Linux gives init, `/dev/console` opened O_RDWR on all three descriptors, and poll(2)'s answer for a terminal. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, b9d9a8f8 and e45008e8: the three descriptors are each open for reading and writing, and the console is writable to poll on any descriptor, a write through a read-only one being EBADF; `fd_hosttest` and pollprobe have rows, with the faults `split-modes` in `fd_stdio_mutate.sh` and `stdio-split-modes` and `console-pollout-writable-only` in `probe_faults.txt`, and the gnu suite logs in as root at the console, starts tmux and requires its pane there (72d31f73), a row that fails under the old modes.

301. tmux on the framebuffer console scrolled the screen each time it drew its status line, so the line showed twice, the bottom row was an empty green bar and every redraw after it landed a line off (2026-10-05, milestone 57's machine, found by the owner; severity P2: tmux was unusable at the console). The console wrapped as soon as a byte reached the last column, and the status line is as wide as the screen; on the last row that wrap scrolled. Linux's console and a VT100 leave the wrap pending until the next printable byte, which their terminfo entries say with `xenl`. Reproduce by replaying tmux's output for TERM=rach2 at 85 by 24 into the console's code, as `fbcon_hosttest.c` includes it: the screen ends a row higher than tmux's own. It breaks the VT100 behavior the console's interpreter follows. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 14128d2d: a byte in the last column leaves the wrap pending, which the next printable byte takes and a cursor move, an erase, BS, HT, LF or CR cancels, and `rach2.ti` has `xenl`; `vga_text_hosttest`'s wrap row fails four of its five checks under the old wrap.

302. `probe_faults.sh` could not catch a fault on x86-64, and one fault's text had gone stale in this milestone (2026-10-05, milestone 57's proofs; severity P3: proofs that could not pass). The script read the boot's log from `build/images`, i686's tree, so an x86-64 run never found the probe's words; and `sigtimedwait-unclamped` still named `kernel_ticks`, which a4114f2b replaced with `rtc_ticks_now()`, and nothing checked the list between proofs. Reproduce with `ARCH=x86_64 sh tests/system/probe_faults.sh stdio-split-modes` at d5a9ced4's parent. It breaks the testing rule that a test is trusted only after it has failed. It depends on milestone 57's build by architecture. Fixed here, 367faa33, 38b25a3b and d5a9ced4: the script takes its paths from the architecture's build tree, the fault names the current text, and `tests/run style` runs `probe_faults.sh --check`, which refuses a list with a fault whose text is not once in its file. The three faults were caught on both architectures.

303. The smp suite's parallel-build row failed once on i686 with every compile done (2026-10-05, the full set after entries 300 to 302; severity P3: a row that judged a round's timing). Sixteen compiles of a few milliseconds each on four processors, between two readings of `/proc/stat`, had to raise every cpuN line's user ticks, which a 100 Hz tick counts; a processor that ended its share between two ticks counted none. Reproduce by running `tests/run smp` until it fails, which earlier sets never did. It breaks the testing rule that a suite judges what the kernel counts and never the clock. It depends on nothing. Fixed here, 0ee388e1: the build runs again, up to five rounds, until every processor has counted user time, and the row fails only when five rounds leave one without.
